We first recall the gentle measurement lemma, which says that if some measurement has a high probability of giving a particular outcome on some state, then the measurement can be implemented in a way so that the state will not be disturbed too much when that outcome occurs.

Lemma 1

(Gentle Measurement Lemma
[
Win99
]
)
.

Let
\rho
be a mixed state acting on
\mathds{C}^{d}
. Let
U
be a unitary and
(\Pi_{0},\Pi_{1}=I-\Pi_{0})
be projectors all acting on
\mathds{C}^{d}\otimes\mathds{C}^{d^{\prime}}
. We interpret
(U,\Pi_{0},\Pi_{1})
as a measurement performed by appending an ancillary system of dimension
d^{\prime}
in the state
\outerproduct{0}{0}
, applying
U
and then performing the projective measurement
\Pi_{0},\Pi_{1}
on the larger system. Assuming that the outcome corresponding to
\Pi_{0}
has probability
1-\epsilon
, we have

\left|{\rho}-{\rho^{\prime}}\right|\leq\sqrt{\epsilon}

where
\rho^{\prime}
is the state after performing the measurement, undoing the unitary
U
and tracing out the ancillary system.

We recall the
hybrid lemma
for reprogramming quantumly-accessible classical oracles.

Theorem 6

(Hybrid Lemma
[
BBBV97
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a quantum algorithm making queries to an oracle
\mathcal{O}
. Let
\ket{\psi_t}=\sum_{w,x,y,t}\alpha_{w,x,y,t}\ket{w,x,y}
denote the joint state of the working register, the query input register and the query output register of the algorithm right before execution of the
t
-th query. For a subset
S
of the domain of
\mathcal{O}
, let
q_{S}(\ket{\psi_t})=\sum_{x\in S}|\alpha_{w,x,y,t}|^{2}
and
q_{S}=\sum_{t}q_{s}(\ket{\psi_t})
, and call
q_{S}
the query weight of
S
.
Let
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
be another oracle whose output differs from
\mathcal{O}
only on points
x\in S
. Then, if
\mathcal{A}
makes
T
queries to the oracle
\mathcal{O}
and
S
is a subset such that
q_{S}\leq\epsilon^{2}/T
, we have
\left|\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}-\outerproduct{\psi'}{\psi'}\right|_{1}\leq 2\epsilon
where
\ket{\psi},\ket{\psi'}
denote the final state of the algorithm
\mathcal{A}
when given access to the oracles
\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
respectively. In the case where
\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O}^{\prime},S
are distributional (along with some auxiliary information register
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux}}
) and possibly correlated, we have
(\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi},\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O}^{\prime},S,\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux}})\approx_{\epsilon}(\outerproduct{\psi'}{\psi'},\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O}^{\prime},S,\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux}})
or equivalently
(\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi},\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O}^{\prime},S,\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux}})\approx_{\sqrt{q_{S}\cdot T}}(\outerproduct{\psi'}{\psi'},\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O}^{\prime},S,\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux}})
.

We recall the following lemma about compressed oracle simulation technique for random oracles. We will use this technique in
Section

7
, thus, we refer the reader to
[
Zha19
]
for details of this technique. As a reminder of the notation, we have
\mathsf{CStO}=\mathsf{StdDecomp}\circ\mathsf{CStO}^{\prime}\circ\mathsf{StdDecomp}\circ\mathsf{Increase}
.

Theorem 7

(Compressed Oracle Lemma
[
Zha19
]
)
.

Consider a quantum algorithm
\mathcal{A}
making queries to a random oracle
H:\{0,1\}^{m}\to\{0,1\}^{n}
and outputting a tuple
(x_{1},\dots,x_{k},y_{1},\dots,y_{k},z)
. Let
R
be a collection of such tuples. Suppose with probability
p
,
\mathcal{A}
outputs a tuple such that (1) the tuple is in
R
and (2)
H(x_{i})=y_{i}
for all
i
. Now consider running the
compressed oracle simulation
of
\mathcal{A}
, suppose the database
D
is measured after
\mathcal{A}
produces its output. Let
p^{\prime}
be the probability that (1) the tuple is in
R
, and (2)
D(x_{i})=y_{i}
for all
i
(and in
particular
D(x_{i})\neq\bot
). Then
\sqrt{p}\leq\sqrt{p^{\prime}}+\sqrt{\frac{k}{2^{n}}}
.

We recall the small-range distributions lemma.

Theorem 8

(Small Range Distributions
[
Zha12
]
)
.

There is a universal constant
C_{\mathsf{SRD}}>1
such that, for any sets
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
, distribution
\mathcal{D}
on
\mathcal{Y}
, any integer
\ell
, and any quantum algorithm
\mathcal{A}
that makes
k
queries, we have

\left|\Pr_{\mathcal{O}\leftarrow\mathfrak{O}_{1}}[\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{O}}()=1]-\Pr_{\mathcal{O}\leftarrow\mathfrak{O}_{2}}[\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{O}}()=1]\right|\leq C_{\mathsf{SRD}}\frac{k^{3}}{\ell}

where the distributions
\mathfrak{O}_{1},\mathfrak{O}_{2}
are defined as follows.

•

For
\mathfrak{O}_{1}
, independently sample
y_{x}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}
for each
x\in\mathcal{X}
and set
\mathcal{O}(x)={y}_{x}
.

•

For
\mathfrak{O}_{2}
, sample a random function
P:\mathcal{X}\to[\ell]
, independently sample
y_{x}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}
for each
x\in[\ell]
and set
\mathcal{O}(x)={y}_{P(x)}
.

Part I
Definitions

4
Quantum Protection Definitions

In this section, we recall the definitions of various
quantum protection
(
[
ÇG24
,
ÇG25
]
) security notions, which is the question of encoding a key/functionality into a quantum state to obtain a classically-impossible security guarantee (such as copy-protection or unbounded leakage-resilience).

4.1
Quantum Protection

We recall the notions of classically unlearnable functionalities and quantum protection schemes.

Definition 6

(Classically Unlearnable Functionalities
[
ÇG24
,
ÇG25
]
)
.

A functionality
\mathcal{F}
is a tuple
(F
,
\mathsf{SampFunc}
,
\mathsf{SampChal}
,
\mathsf{Pred})
whose elements are defined as follows.

•

F
: A sequence consisting of sets (for each
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
) of classical circuits of polynomial size.

•

\mathsf{SampFunc}(1^{\lambda}):
An interactive efficient (quantum) algorithm that outputs a value
f\in F
at the end of the interaction.

•

\mathsf{SampChal}(f)
: An efficient (quantum) algorithm that takes as input a value
f\in F
and outputs a challenge input
x
and an answer key
ak
.

•

\mathsf{Pred}(f,ak,x,y)
: An efficient (quantum) algorithm that takes as input a value
f\in F
, an answer key
ak
, an input
x
and a value
y
, and outputs
1
or
0
, denoting if
y
is a valid answer for input
x
with respect to
f
and
ak
or not.

Consider the following between a challenger and an adversary
\mathcal{A}
.

\mathsf{LEARNING-GAME}_{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda})

1.

The challenger and the adversary interact where the challenger simply simulates
\mathsf{SampFunc}
. Let
f^{*}
denote the output of
\mathsf{SampFunc}
at the end of the interaction.

2.

For any polynomial number of rounds: The adversary
\mathcal{A}
submits the classical description of a oracular quantum circuit
C
with classical output, the challenger executes
C
with oracle access to
f^{*}
and submits the outcome to the adversary.

3.

The challenger samples a challenge input
x^{*},ak^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{SampChal}(f^{*})
and submits
x^{*}
to the adversary
\mathcal{A}
.

4.

\mathcal{A}
outputs
y^{*}
.

5.

The challenger outputs
1
if and only if
\mathsf{Pred}(f^{*},ak^{*},x^{*},y^{*})
outputs
1
.

A functionality
\mathcal{F}
is said to satisfy
p_{triv}
-classical-unlearnability
if for any QPT adversary
\mathcal{A}
,

\Pr[\mathsf{LEARNING-GAME}_{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda})=1]\leq p_{triv}(\lambda)+\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

Note that for any non-trivial game (i.e.
p_{triv}
is not close to
1
), the size of the challenge
x^{*}
must be superlogarithmic.

Definition 7

(Quantum Protection Scheme
[
ÇG24
,
ÇG25
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}=(F,\mathsf{SampFunc},\mathsf{SampChal},\mathsf{Pred})
be a functionality. A quantum protection scheme
\mathsf{QuantumProtection}
for
\mathcal{F}
consists of the following efficient algorithms.

•

\mathsf{QuantumProtection}.\mathsf{Protect}(1^{\lambda},f)
: On input a circuit
f\in{F}
, outputs a quantum register
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}
.

•

\mathsf{QuantumProtection}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}},x)
: On input a key register and a circuit input
x
, outputs an evaluation.

We require evaluation correctness: For each
f\in F
and input
x
to
f
,

\Pr[y=f(x):\begin{array}[]{c}\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}\leftarrow\mathsf{QuantumProtection}.\mathsf{Protect}(1^{\lambda},f)\\
y\leftarrow\mathsf{QuantumProtection}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}},x)\end{array}]\geq 1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

4.2
LOCC Leakage-Resilience

We recall LOCC leakage-resilience security for general functionalities.

Definition 8

(LOCC Leakage-Resilience Security
[
ÇGLZR24
,
ÇG24
,
ÇG25
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}=(F
,
\mathsf{SampFunc}
,
\mathsf{SampChal}
,
\mathsf{Pred})
be a functionality that is
p_{triv}
-classical-unlearnable and let
\mathsf{QuantumProtection}
be a quantum protection scheme for
\mathcal{F}
. Consider the following game between the challenger and an adversary
\mathcal{A}
.

\mathsf{LEAK-GAME}_{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda})

1.

The challenger and the adversary
\mathcal{A}
interact where the challenger simply simulates
\mathsf{SampFunc}
. Let
f^{*}
denote the output of
\mathsf{SampFunc}
at the end of the interaction.

2.

The challenger samples
\mathsf{R}_{0}\leftarrow\mathsf{QuantumProtection}.\mathsf{Protect}(1^{\lambda},f^{*})
.

3.

Leakage Phase:
Then, for any number
13
13

13

Note that when
\mathcal{A}
is QPT, implicitly this will be any
polynomial
number of rounds.
of rounds, the following is executed (starting with
i=1
).

1.

\mathcal{A}
specifies a quantum leakage circuit
E_{i}
that takes as input a quantum register and outputs a classical string and the updated quantum register.

2.

The challenger executes
L_{i},\mathsf{R}_{i}\leftarrow E_{i}(\mathsf{R}_{i-1})
and submits the classical string
L_{i}
to
\mathcal{A}
. Then
i
is incremented.

4.

The challenger samples a challenge input
x^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{SampChal}(f^{*})
and submits
x^{*}
to the adversary
\mathcal{A}
.

5.

\mathcal{A}
outputs
y^{*}
.

6.

The challenger outputs
1
if and only if
\mathsf{Pred}(f^{*},ak^{*},x^{*},y^{*})
outputs
1
.

A quantum protection scheme
\mathsf{QuantumProtection}
is said to satisfy
LOCC leakage-resilience
for the functionality
\mathcal{F}
if for any QPT adversary
\mathcal{A}
,

\Pr[\mathsf{LEAK-GAME}_{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda})=1]\leq p_{triv}(\lambda)+\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

Note that previous works also define unbounded (i.e. 1-round) leakage-resilience. In this work, since we achieve the strongest notion, LOCC leakage-resilience, we will not define unbounded leakage-resilience.

In
Appendix

A
, for ease of exposition, we also explicitly write out the special case of this definition for digital signatures and public-key encryption explicitly, since these are the two simplest and most natural use cases.

4.3
One-Shot Signatures

We recall one-shot signatures.

Definition 9

(One-Shot Signatures
[
AGKZ20
]
)
.

A one-shot signature scheme
\mathsf{OSS}
consists of the following efficient algorithms.

•

\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
: Takes in a security parameter, outputs a common reference string
crs
.

•

\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}(crs)
: Takes in the CRS value
crs
, outputs a verification key
vk
and a quantum signing key
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}
.

•

\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}(crs,vk,\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}},m)
: Takes in the CRS value,a verification key, a signing key
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}
, and a message
m
; outputs a classical signature
sig
.

•

\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}(crs,vk,m,sig)
: Takes in the CRS value
crs
, a verification key
vk
, a message
m
and a signature
sig
,; outputs
0
or
1
.

Correctness:

For any message
m\in\mathcal{M}
,

\Pr[out_{\mathsf{Ver}}=1:\begin{array}[]{c}crs\leftarrow\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
vk,\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}\leftarrow\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}(crs)\\
sig\leftarrow\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}(crs,vk,\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}},m)\\
out_{\mathsf{Ver}}\leftarrow\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}(crs,vk,m,sig)\end{array}]\geq 1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

One-Shot Security:

For any QPT adversary
\mathcal{A}
,

\Pr[\begin{array}[]{c}m_{1}\neq m_{2}\\
\wedge\\
out_{1,\mathsf{Ver}}=1\\
\wedge\\
out_{2,\mathsf{Ver}}=1\end{array}:\begin{array}[]{c}crs\leftarrow\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
vk,sig_{1},m_{1},sig_{2},m_{2}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}(crs)\\
out_{1,\mathsf{Ver}}\leftarrow\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}(crs,vk,m_{1},sig_{1})\\
out_{2,\mathsf{Ver}}\leftarrow\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}(crs,vk,m_{2},sig_{2})\end{array}]\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

In
Section

6
, we introduce a new security notion called incompressibility for one-shot signature in the classical oracle model. Thus, we also define OSS in the oracle model below
14
14

14

This is slightly different from the standard oracle model where there is a single global oracle (rather than an oracle being output for each instance of the scheme) that all the algorithms and adversaries query. We use this model for simplicity, and they are equivalent to each other.
.

Definition 10

(One-Shot Signatures in the Classical Oracle Model)
.

A one-shot signature scheme
\mathsf{OSS}
in the classical oracle model consists of the following efficient algorithms.

•

\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenOracles}(1^{\lambda})
: Takes in a security parameter, outputs a classical oracle
\mathcal{O}
.

•

\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}^{(\cdot)}(1^{\lambda})
: An oracle algorithm that takes in a security parameter, outputs a verification key
vk
and a quantum signing key
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}
.

•

\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}^{(\cdot)}(vk,\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}},m)
: An oracle algorithm that takes in a verification key, a signing key
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}
, and a message
m
; outputs a classical signature
sig
.

•

\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}^{(\cdot)}(vk,m,sig)
: An oracle algorithm that takes in a verification key
vk
, a message
m
and a signature
sig
,; outputs
0
or
1
.

Correctness:

For any message
m\in\mathcal{M}
,

\Pr[out_{\mathsf{Ver}}=1:\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{O}\leftarrow\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenOracles}(1^{\lambda})\\
vk,\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}\leftarrow\mathsf{OSS}^{\mathcal{O}}.\mathsf{GenQKey}(1^{\lambda})\\
sig\leftarrow\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}^{\mathcal{O}}(vk,\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}},m)\\
out_{\mathsf{Ver}}\leftarrow\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}^{\mathcal{O}}(vk,m,sig)\end{array}]\geq 1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

One-Shot Security:

For any query-bounded adversary
\mathcal{A}
,

\Pr[\begin{array}[]{c}m_{1}\neq m_{2}\\
\wedge\\
out_{1,\mathsf{Ver}}=1\\
\wedge\\
out_{2,\mathsf{Ver}}=1\end{array}:\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{O}\leftarrow\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenOracles}(1^{\lambda})\\
vk,sig_{1},m_{1},sig_{2},m_{2}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{O}}(1^{\lambda})\\
out_{1,\mathsf{Ver}}\leftarrow\mathsf{OSS}^{\mathcal{O}}.\mathsf{Ver}(vk,m_{1},sig_{1})\\
out_{2,\mathsf{Ver}}\leftarrow\mathsf{OSS}^{\mathcal{O}}.\mathsf{Ver}(vk,m_{2},sig_{2})\end{array}]\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

We define strong one-shot security similarly, with the difference that it is only required to have
sig_{1}\neq sig_{2}
(and not necessarily
m_{1}\neq m_{2}
).

5
Definitional Work on Quantum Fire

In this section, we recall some existing definitions related to quantum fire, and also introduce definitions for new primitives and security notions.

5.1
Quantum Fire

We first recall quantum fire (
[
BNZ25
]
). Throughout the paper, we will only consider public-key quantum fire even when we ignore writing out
public-key
explicitly.

Definition 11

(Quantum Fire)
.

A (public-key) quantum fire scheme
\mathsf{QFire}
consists of the following efficient algorithms.

•

\mathsf{QFire}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
: Takes in a security parameter, outputs a public key
pk
and a secret key
sk
.

•

\mathsf{QFire}.\mathsf{Spark}(sk)
: Takes in the secret key, outputs a serial number
sn
, and a
flame state
in the register
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{flame}}
.

•

\mathsf{QFire}.\mathsf{Clone}(pk,sn,\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{flame}})
: Takes in the public key, a serial number, a flame state register
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{flame}}
, outputs a new flame register
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{clone}}
(along with the input register
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{flame}}
).

•

\mathsf{QFire}.\mathsf{Ver}(pk,sn,\mathsf{R})
: Takes in the public key, a serial number, and an alleged flame state, outputs
0
or
1
.

We require the following correctness guarantees.

Verification Correctness:

\Pr[out_{\mathsf{Ver}}=1:\begin{array}[]{c}pk,sk\leftarrow\mathsf{QFire}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
sn,\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{flame}}\leftarrow\mathsf{QFire}.\mathsf{Spark}(sk)\\
out_{\mathsf{Ver}}\leftarrow\mathsf{QFire}.\mathsf{Ver}(pk,sn,\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{flame}})\\
\end{array}]\geq 1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

Cloning Correctness:

For any polynomial
p(\cdot)
,

\Pr[\forall i\in[p(\lambda)]~out_{i,\mathsf{Ver}}=1:\begin{array}[]{c}pk,sk\leftarrow\mathsf{QFire}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
sn,\mathsf{R}_{1}\leftarrow\mathsf{QFire}.\mathsf{Spark}(sk)\\
\text{For }i=2,\dots,p(\lambda)~\mathsf{R}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{QFire}.\mathsf{Clone}(pk,sn,\mathsf{R}_{i-1})\\
\text{For }i=1,\dots,p(\lambda)~out_{i,\mathsf{Ver}}\leftarrow\mathsf{QFire}.\mathsf{Ver}(pk,sn,\mathsf{R}_{i})\end{array}]\geq 1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

We introduce a stronger correctness condition called
strong cloning correctness
.

Definition 12

(Strong Cloning Correctness)
.

A quantum fire scheme
\mathsf{QFire}
is said to satisfy
strong cloning correctness
if the following are satisfied: There exists a collection of pure states
\ket{\psi_{sk,sn}}
such that with probability
1
the output of
\mathsf{QFire}.\mathsf{Spark}(sk)
is
sn,\ket{\psi_{sk, sn}}
for some
sn
. Let
M_{sk,sn}
denote the binary outcome projective measurement onto
\ket{\psi_{sk, sn}}
.

\Pr[b_{1}=b_{2}=1:\begin{array}[]{c}pk,sk\leftarrow\mathsf{QFire}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
sn,\mathsf{R}_{flame}\leftarrow\mathsf{QFire}.\mathsf{Spark}(sk)\\
\mathsf{R}_{clone}\leftarrow\mathsf{QFire}.\mathsf{Clone}(pk,sn,\mathsf{R}_{flame})\\
b_{1}\leftarrow M_{sk,sn}(\mathsf{R}_{flame})\\
b_{2}\leftarrow M_{sk,sn}(\mathsf{R}_{clone})\end{array}]\geq 1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

Note that in oracle-based constructions, the flame states also depend on the oracle itself
15
15

15

Equivalently, we can consider the whole oracle as part of the secret key for purposes of this definition.
in addition to
sk,sn
.

We now recall untelegraphability security for quantum fire. We slightly extend the definition of
[
BNZ25
]
to consider collusion-resistant quantum fire, where the adversary gets multiple independent flame states.

Definition 13

(Untelegraphability Security)
.

Consider the following game between an adversary
\mathcal{A}=(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2})
and a challenger.

\mathsf{TELEGRAPH-GAME}_{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda})

1.

The challenger samples
pk,sk\leftarrow\mathsf{QFire}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
and submits
pk
to
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
.

2.

For polynomially many number of rounds: The adversary sends an empty query, and the challenger samples
sn,\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{flame}}\leftarrow\mathsf{QFire}.\mathsf{Spark}(sk)
and submits
sn,\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{flame}}
to the adversary
\mathcal{A}_{1}
.

3.

The adversary
\mathcal{A}_{2}
outputs a message
T
.

4.

The adversary
\mathcal{A}_{1}
receives
T
and outputs a classical string
L
.

5.

The adversary
\mathcal{A}_{2}
receives
L
, outputs
sn^{*},\mathsf{R}^{\prime}
.

6.

The challenger outputs
1
if
b=1
where
b\leftarrow\mathsf{QFire}.\mathsf{Ver}(pk,sn^{*},\mathsf{R}^{\prime})=1
, and otherwise it outputs
0
.

We say that the quantum fire scheme
\mathsf{QFire}
satisfies
untelegraphability security
if for any for any polynomial-time (or query-bounded, for unconditional oracle-based schemes) adversary
\mathcal{A}
, we have

\Pr[\mathsf{TELEGRAPH-GAME}_{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda})=1]\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

Note that the weaker notion of
non-adaptive untelegraphability security
can also be defined, where
\mathcal{A}_{1}
produces its output without receiving a message from
\mathcal{A}_{2}
.

Finally, we introduce the notion of mini-scheme for quantum fire, analogous to the notion of mini-scheme for quantum money. This definition makes proofs easier, and it is easy to see that any mini-scheme for quantum fire can generically be upgraded to a full scheme using digital signatures (
Definition

4
), simply by signing the serial number (signature is made part of the new serial number) and verifying the signature on the serial number as part of verification - just like in the case of upgrading quantum money mini-schemes to full schemes (
[
AC12
]
).

Definition 14

(Quantum Fire Mini-Scheme)
.

A public-key quantum fire mini-scheme
\mathsf{QFireMini}
consists of the following efficient algorithms.

•

\mathsf{QFireMini}.\mathsf{Spark}(1^{\lambda})
: Takes in the security parameter, outputs a serial number
sn
, and a
flame state

\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{flame}}
.

•

\mathsf{QFireMini}.\mathsf{Clone}(sn,\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{flame}})
: Takes in a serial number, a flame register, outputs a new flame register
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{clone}}
(along with the input register
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{flame}}
).

•

\mathsf{QFireMini}.\mathsf{Ver}(sn,\mathsf{R})
: Takes in a serial number, and an alleged flame state, outputs
0
or
1
.

Verification Correctness:

\Pr[out_{\mathsf{Ver}}=1:\begin{array}[]{c}sn,\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{flame}}\leftarrow\mathsf{QFireMini}.\mathsf{Spark}(1^{\lambda})\\
out_{\mathsf{Ver}}\leftarrow\mathsf{QFireMini}.\mathsf{Ver}(sn,\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{flame}})\\
\end{array}]\geq 1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

Strong Cloning Correctness:

We require that there exists a collection of pure states
\ket{\psi_{k}}
such that with probability
1
the output of
\mathsf{QFireMini}.\mathsf{Spark}(1^{\lambda})
is
sn,\ket{\psi_{k}}
for some
k,sn
and

\Pr[b_{1}=b_{2}=1:\begin{array}[]{c}sn,\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{flame}}\leftarrow\mathsf{QFireMini}.\mathsf{Spark}(1^{\lambda})\\
\mathsf{R}_{clone}\leftarrow\mathsf{QFireMini}.\mathsf{Clone}(sn,\mathsf{R}_{flame})\\
b_{1}\leftarrow M_{k}(\mathsf{R}_{flame})\\
b_{2}\leftarrow M_{k}(\mathsf{R}_{clone})\end{array}]\geq 1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

where
M_{k}
denotes the binary outcome projective measurement onto
\ket{\psi_{k}}
.

Note that in oracle-based constructions, the flame states also depend on the oracle itself
16
16

16

Equivalently, we can consider the whole oracle as part of the key
k
for purposes of this definition.
in addition to
k
.

Definition 15

(Untelegraphability Security for Quantum Fire Mini-Scheme)
.

Consider the following game between an adversary
\mathcal{A}=(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2})
and a challenger.

\mathsf{MINI-TELEGRAPH-GAME}_{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda})

1.

The challenger samples
sn,\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{flame}}\leftarrow\mathsf{QFireMini}.\mathsf{Spark}(1^{\lambda})
and submits
sn,\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{flame}}
to the adversary
\mathcal{A}_{1}
.

2.

The adversary
\mathcal{A}_{2}
receives
sn
and outputs a message
T
.

3.

The adversary
\mathcal{A}_{1}
receives
T
and outputs a classical string
L
.

4.

The adversary
\mathcal{A}_{2}
receives
L
, outputs
\mathsf{R}^{\prime}
.

5.

The challenger executes
\mathsf{QFireMini}.\mathsf{Ver}(sn,\mathsf{R}^{\prime})
outputs if it outputs
1
, otherwise it outputs
0
.

We say that the quantum fire scheme
\mathsf{QFireMini}
satisfies
untelegraphability security
if for any QPT (or query bounded, in case of unconditional oracle-based schemes) adversary
\mathcal{A}
, we have

\Pr[\mathsf{MINI-TELEGRAPH-GAME}_{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda})=1]\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

5.2
Interactive Untelegraphability Security

We introduce the notion of interactive telegraphing security, inspired by the notion of
LOCC leakage-resilience
(
Definition

8
) defined by
[
ÇGLZR24
]
. For simplicity, we will explicitly define it only for mini-schemes, but the collusion-resistant case is defined similarly to
Definition

13
(where
\mathcal{A}_{1}
receives multiple independent flame states).

Definition 16

(Interactive Untelegraphability Security)
.

Consider the following game between an adversary
\mathcal{A}=(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2})
and a challenger.

\mathsf{MINI-INTERACTIVE-TELEGRAPH-GAME}_{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda})

1.

The challenger samples
sn,\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{flame}}\leftarrow\mathsf{QFireMini}.\mathsf{Spark}(1^{\lambda})
and submits
sn,\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{flame}}
to the adversary
\mathcal{A}_{1}
.

2.

The adversaries
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
interact classically by exchanging classical messages for any number of rounds
17
17

17

Note that when
\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2}
are QPT, implicitly this will be any
polynomial
number of rounds.
.

3.

The adversary
\mathcal{A}_{2}
receives
L
, outputs
\mathsf{R}^{\prime}
.

4.

The challenger executes
\mathsf{QFireMini}.\mathsf{Ver}(sn,\mathsf{R}^{\prime})
outputs if it outputs
1
, otherwise it outputs
0
.

We say that the quantum fire scheme
\mathsf{QFireMini}
satisfies
interactive untelegraphability security
if for any QPT (or query bounded, in case of unconditional oracle-based schemes) adversary
\mathcal{A}
, we have

\Pr[\mathsf{MINI-INTERACTIVE-TELEGRAPH-GAME}_{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda})=1]\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

5.3
Quantum Key-Fire

We now introduce a new notion called
quantum key-fire
. Quantum key-fire is the functional version of quantum fire, where the flame state can be used to evaluate a functionality, and we require
unbounded/LOCC leakage-resilience
(
[
ÇGLZR24
]
) as our security notion, which is a generalization of the notion of untelegraphability. Quantum key-fire is to quantum fire what copy-protection (
[
ALL+21
,
CLLZ21
]
) is to public-key quantum money. Just like the case of copy-protection and quantum money, it is easy to see that quantum key-fire implies quantum fire.

A quantum key-fire scheme is an
quantum protection scheme
(
Definition

7
) with unbounded/LOCC leakage-resilience, with the addition of a cloning algorithm. We write the full definition for reference.

Definition 17

(Quantum Key-Fire)
.

A public-key quantum key-fire scheme for a functionality
\mathcal{F}
(
Definition

6
) consists of the following efficient algorithms.

•

\mathsf{QKeyFire}.\mathsf{Protect}(1^{\lambda},f)
: Takes in a function
f\in F
and outputs a
flame state

\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}
.

•

\mathsf{QKeyFire}.\mathsf{Clone}(\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}})
: Takes in a flame register
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}
, outputs a new register
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{clone}}
(along with the input register
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}
).

•

\mathsf{QKeyFire}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}},x):
Takes in a flame register
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}
, and an input
x
, outputs a value.

Evaluation Correctness:

For all
f\in F
, for all
x
,

\Pr[y=f(x):\begin{array}[]{c}\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}\leftarrow\mathsf{QKeyFire}.\mathsf{Protect}(1^{\lambda},f)\\
y\leftarrow\mathsf{QKeyFire}.\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}},x)\end{array}]\geq 1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

Strong Cloning Correctness:

We require that there exists a collection of pure states
\ket{\psi_{k,f}}
such that for all
f\in F
, with probability
1
the output of
\mathsf{QKeyFire}.\mathsf{Protect}(1^{\lambda},f)
is
\ket{\psi_{k,f}}
for some
k
and

\Pr[b_{1}=b_{2}=1:\begin{array}[]{c}k,sn,\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{flame}}\leftarrow\mathsf{QKeyFire}.\mathsf{Protect}(1^{\lambda},f)\\
\mathsf{R}_{clone}\leftarrow\mathsf{QKeyFire}.\mathsf{Clone}(\mathsf{R}_{flame})\\
b_{1}\leftarrow M_{k,f}(\mathsf{R}_{flame})\\
b_{2}\leftarrow M_{k,f}(\mathsf{R}_{clone})\end{array}]\geq 1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

where
M_{k,f}
denotes the binary outcome projective measurement onto
\ket{\psi_{k,f}}
.

Note that in oracle-based constructions, the flame states also depend on the oracle itself
18
18

18

Equivalently, we can consider the whole oracle as part of the key
k
for purposes of this definition.
in addition to
f,k
.

Security:

When
\mathsf{QKeyFire}
is a key-fire scheme for some (classically-unlearnable) functionality
\mathcal{F}
(
Definition

6
), we will require that
\mathsf{QKeyFire}
satisfies LOCC leakage-resilience (
Definition

8
) for
\mathcal{F}
.

5.3.1
Quantum Key-Fire for Signing

In this section, we provide the explicit definition of the special case of quantum-key fire for signing, since this the main functionality we consider in this work.

Definition 18

(Quantum Key-Fire for Signing)
.

A public-key quantum key-fire scheme for signing
\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}
consists of the following efficient algorithms.

•

\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
: Takes in a security parameter, outputs a verification key (also called serial number)
vk
, and a
key state

\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{flame}}
.

•

\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}.\mathsf{Clone}(vk,\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}})
: Takes in a key register
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}
, outputs a new register
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{clone}}
along with the input register
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}
.

•

\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}.\mathsf{Sign}(vk,\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}},m):
Takes in a key register and a message
m
, outputs a signature.

•

\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}.\mathsf{Ver}(vk,m,sig)
: Takes in a verification key (also called serial number), a message and an alleged signature, outputs
1
or
0
.

Evaluation Correctness:

For all
m\in\mathcal{M}
,

\Pr[\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}.\mathsf{Ver}(vk,m,sig)=1:\begin{array}[]{c}vk,\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}\leftarrow\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
sig\leftarrow\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}.\mathsf{Sign}(vk,\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}},m)\end{array}]\geq 1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

Strong cloning correctness:

This is defined the same as before: We require that output of
\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
is a pure state
\ket{\psi_{k}}
and that clone on input
\ket{\psi_{k}}
outputs two copies,
\ket{\psi_{k}}\otimes\ket{\psi_{k}}
.

Definition 19

(LOCC Leakage-Resilience Security for Quantum Key-Fire for Signing)
.

Consider the following game between an adversary
\mathcal{A}
and a challenger.

\mathsf{LR-SIGN-GAME}_{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda})

1.

The challenger samples
vk,\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}\leftarrow\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
and submits
vk
to
\mathcal{A}
.

2.

Set
\mathsf{R}_{0}=\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}
.

3.

Leakage Phase:
For any number
19
19

19

Note that when
\mathcal{A}
is QPT, implicitly this will be any
polynomial
number of rounds.
of rounds, the following is executed (starting with
i=1
).

1.

\mathcal{A}
specifies a quantum leakage circuit
E_{i}
that takes as input a quantum register and outputs a classical string and the updated quantum register.

2.

The challenger executes
L_{i},\mathsf{R}_{i}\leftarrow E_{i}(\mathsf{R}_{i-1})
and submits the classical string
L_{i}
to
\mathcal{A}
. Then
i
is incremented.

4.

The challenger samples a random message
m^{*}\leftarrow\mathcal{M}
.

5.

The adversary
\mathcal{A}
receives
m^{*}
, outputs
sig^{*}
.

6.

The challenger checks if
\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}.\mathsf{Ver}(sn,m^{*},sig^{*})=1
. If so, it outputs
1
, otherwise it outputs
0
.

We say that the quantum key-fire scheme
\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}
satisfies
LOCC leakage-resilience security
if for any QPT (or query bounded, in case of unconditional oracle-based schemes) adversary
\mathcal{A}
, we have

\Pr[\mathsf{LR-SIGN-GAME}_{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda})=1]\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

Part II
Technical Tools

6
One-Shot Signatures with Incompressibility Security

In this section, we introduce a new notion called
incompressibility
security for one-shot signatures in the classical oracle model, and then construct a scheme that satisfies it. The section is organized as follows. First we introduce our definition of incompressibility and give our theorem statement about existence of an incompressible OSS scheme. Then in
Section

6.1
, we introduce a technical tool called
operational incompressibility
that simplifies the proof of our incompressibility notion. Then we give our construction in
Section

6.2
and prove its incompressibility property in
Section

6.4
. In
Section

6.3
, we show some technical results related to subspace oracles that will be used in our proof in
Section

6.4
and might be of independent interest.

For our incompressibility notion, we consider a one-shot signature scheme
\mathsf{OSS}
with
split oracles.

Definition 20

(One-Shot Signature Scheme with Split Oracles)
.

Let
\mathsf{OSS}
be a one-shot signature scheme with classical oracles (
Definition

10
). Write
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}}
to denote its oracle.
\mathsf{OSS}
is said to have
split oracles
if
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}}
is a tuple of (correlated) oracles
(\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}}
,
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}}
,
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}})=\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}}
, where each algorithm of
\mathsf{OSS}
uses only the corresponding oracle (e.g.
\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}
only uses
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}}
). We also assume that the inputs to
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}}
and
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}}
are of the form
vk,x
where
vk
denotes a verification key.

Note that any one-shot signature scheme can trivially be converted into a scheme with split oracles by setting all three oracles to be the full oracle, but this will not satisfy incompressibility.

Now we define incompressibility.

Definition 21

(Incompressibility)
.

\mathsf{OSS}
is said to satisfy
incompressibility security
for
\mathcal{O}\in\{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}}\}
if the following is satisfied. There exists some
c>0
such that for any query-bounded adversary
\mathcal{A}_{1}
with unbounded length classical output, there exists a mapping
\mathcal{M}
(which outputs a list
20
20

20

We will refer to this list as the incompressibility list.

\mathfrak{L}
with at most
s(\lambda)
elements where
s(\lambda)=(q(\lambda))^{c}
and
q(\lambda)
is the number of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}}
queries made by
\mathcal{A}_{1}
) such that for all query-bounded
\mathcal{A}_{2}
, we have

\mathds{E}\left[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}vk,x:\\
\mathcal{O}(vk,x)\neq 0\\
vk\not\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}},L,\mathcal{A}_{2})\end{subarray}}\mathsf{QW}^{(vk,x)}_{\mathcal{A}_{2},L,\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}}}:\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}}\leftarrow\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenOracles}(1^{\lambda})\\
L\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{1}^{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}}}(1^{\lambda})\end{array}\right]\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

where
\mathsf{QW}^{(vk,x)}_{\mathcal{A}_{2},L,\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}}}
denotes the total query weight of
\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}}}(L)
to the oracle
\mathcal{O}
on oracle input
vk,x
, and
\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2}
are allowed to share an unbounded length classical randomness in advance (i.e. before oracles are sampled).

If incompressibility is satisfied for both
\mathcal{O}\in\{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}}\}
, then we will simply say that OSS satisfies incompressibility.

We define
strong incompressibility
similarly, where we require the list
\mathcal{M}
to output pairs
(vk,z)
and the summation above is taken over
(vk,z)\not\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}},L,\mathcal{A}_{2})
. It is easy to see that strong incompressibility for
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}}
follows from incompressibility for
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}}
and strong one-shot security.

We claim that a one-shot signature scheme with incompressibility security exists.

Theorem 9
.

Relative to a classical oracle, there exists a one-shot signature scheme for
1
-bit messages that satisfies one-shot security and incompressibility security.

We give our construction in
Section

6.2
and prove its incompressibility in
Section

6.4
.

6.1
Operational Incompressibility

As a technical tool for proving incompressibility, we define the notion of operational incompressibility.

Definition 22

(Operational Incompressibility)
.

\mathsf{OSS}
is said to satisfy
operational incompressibility security
for
\mathcal{O}\in\{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}}\}
if the following is satisfied. There exists some
c>0
such that for any query-bounded adversary
\mathcal{A}_{1}
with unbounded-length classical output, for all query-bounded
\mathcal{A}_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr[\begin{array}[]{c}\forall i\in[s(\lambda)+1]~\mathcal{O}(vk_{i},x_{i})\neq 0\\
\wedge\\
(vk_{i})_{i\in[s(\lambda)+1]}~\text{distinct}\end{array}:\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}}\leftarrow\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenOracles}(1^{\lambda})\\
L\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{1}^{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}}}(1^{\lambda})\\
(vk_{i},x_{i})_{i\in[s(\lambda)+1]}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}}}(L)\end{array}]\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

where
s(\lambda)=(q(\lambda))^{c}
and
q(\lambda)
is the number of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}}
queries made by the adversary
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2}
are allowed to share an unbounded length classical randomness in advance (i.e. before oracles are sampled).

Theorem 10
.

If an OSS scheme satisfies operational incompressibility, then it also satisfies incompressibility.

Now we prove this theorem.
Let
\mathsf{OSS}
be a scheme that satisfies operational incompressibility. Fix
\mathcal{O}\in\{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}}\}
. For any
vk
, define

q_{vk}=\sum_{x:\mathcal{O}(vk,x)\neq 0}\mathsf{QW}^{vk,x}_{\mathcal{A}_{2},L,\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}}}

The dependence on
\mathcal{A}_{2},L,\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}}
are implicit.

Let
vk_{1},\dots,vk_{s},vk_{s+1}
be the inputs such that

•

q_{{vk}_{i}}\geq q_{{vk}_{i+1}}
for
i\in[s]

•

q_{{vk}_{s+1}}\geq q_{{vk}^{\prime}}
for any
{vk}^{\prime}\not\in\{{vk}_{i}\}_{i\in[s+1]}

That is,
{vk}_{i}
are the inputs with the highest
valid
query weights. Note that these all implicitly depend on
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}},L,\mathcal{A}_{2}
.

We set
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}},L,\mathcal{A}_{2})
to be
\{{vk}_{1},\dots,{vk}_{s}\}
. Now suppose for a contradiction that incompressibility property is not satisfied, that is, there exists some
\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2}
and polynomial
t(\cdot)
such that for infinitely many
\lambda>0
,

p_{out}=\mathds{E}\left[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{vk}:\\
{vk}\not\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}},L,\mathcal{A}_{2})\end{subarray}}q_{vk}:\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}}\leftarrow\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenOracles}(1^{\lambda})\\
L\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{1}^{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}}}(1^{\lambda})\end{array}\right]\geq\frac{1}{t(\lambda)}.

Let
u(\lambda)
denote the number of queries to
\mathcal{O}
by
\mathcal{A}_{2}
. Consider the following adversary
\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\prime}
for the operational incompressibility game.

\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\prime}

1.

Set
D=\emptyset
.

2.

For
n(\lambda)=u(\lambda)\cdot t(\lambda)\cdot s(\lambda)\cdot\lambda^{2}\cdot(s(\lambda)+1)
times (in
s+1

groups
):

1.

Simulate
\mathcal{A}_{2}(L)
, but measure a random query to
\mathcal{O}
to obtain
vk,x
.

2.

If
\mathcal{O}({vk},x)\neq 0
and
vk\not\in D
21
21

21

Here we mean that there is no
x
such that
({vk},x)\in D
, add
({vk},x)
to
D
and skip to the next
group
.

3.

Output
D
.

We will show that we can violate operational incompressibility using
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{2}
. We continue our proof by considering two cases/events. Define
E_{1}
to be the event that
q_{{vk}_{s+1}}\geq\frac{1}{t(\lambda)\cdot s(\lambda)\cdot\lambda}
, and
E_{2}
to be its complement. Thus we have either
\Pr[E_{1}]\geq\frac{1}{4\cdot t\cdot u-2}
or
\Pr[E_{2}]\geq 1-\frac{1}{4\cdot t\cdot u-2}
.

Now we consider the case
\Pr[E_{1}]\geq\frac{1}{4\cdot t\cdot u-2}
. We claim that
\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\prime}
wins the operational incompressibility game with probability at least
\frac{1}{8\cdot t\cdot u-4}
. We condition on the event
E_{1}
, and we will show that the adversaries will win with probability
\geq 1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
. Observe that for each
{vk}_{i}
with
i\in[s+1]
, we have that the probability of
{vk}_{i}\in D
is
1-\exp{-\frac{n}{u \cdot t\cdot s \cdot\lambda}}\geq 1-\exp{-\lambda}
since the weight of each
{vk}_{i}
is at least
\frac{1}{t\cdot s\cdot\lambda}
. Thus, by the union bound, we get that
{vk}_{1},\dots,{vk}_{s+1}\in D
with overwhelming probability, which indeed violates incompressibility security.

Now we consider the case
\Pr[E_{2}]\geq 1-\frac{1}{4\cdot t\cdot u-2}
. Let
\mathsf{GOOD}
be the event that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{vk}:{vk}\not\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}},L,\mathcal{A}_{2})\end{subarray}}q_{vk}\geq\frac{1}{2\cdot t}

Since expectation of this value (
p_{out}
) is greater than
\frac{1}{t(\lambda)}
and since
\Pr[E_{2}]\geq 1-\frac{1}{4\cdot t\cdot u-2}
, we get
\Pr[E_{2}\wedge\mathsf{GOOD}]\geq\frac{1}{4\cdot t\cdot u-2}
by an application of Markov’s inequality and union bound.

We claim that
\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\prime}
wins the operational incompressibility game with overwhelming probability conditioned on
E_{2}\wedge\mathsf{GOOD}
, which happens with non-negligible probability. Let
G
denote the complement set of
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{A}_{2},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}},L)
. Let
{vk}^{*}_{1},\dots,{vk}^{*}_{s+1}
be the values added to the database
D
during each group during execution of
\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\prime}
, in the order of addition. In particular, if no item was added during a group, we set
{vk}^{*}_{i}=\bot
. We now claim that with overwhelming probability,
{vk}^{*}_{1},\dots,{vk}^{*}_{s+1}\neq\bot
. Note that once we establish this, we will have shown that we can violate operational incompressibility with non-negligible probability. Now to prove our claim, first observe that
{vk}^{*}_{1}\in G
with overwhelming probability since
q_{G}:=\sum_{{vk}\in G}q_{vk}\geq\frac{1}{2t}
(since we conditioned on
\mathsf{GOOD}
). Then, we claim that
{vk}^{*}_{2}\neq\bot
with overwhelming probability. To see this, observe that
q_{G}\geq\frac{1}{2t}
and for any possible value of
{vk}_{1}^{*}\in G
, we have
q_{{vk}_{1}^{*}}\leq\frac{1}{t\cdot s\cdot\lambda}
(since we conditioned on
E_{2}
) and thus
q_{G}-q_{{vk}_{1}^{*}}\geq\frac{1}{2t}
. Continuing like this, we can show that each
{vk}_{i}^{*}
is
\in G
with overwhelming probability, no matter what the previous values
{vk}_{1}^{*},\dots,{vk}_{i-1}^{*}
are. This completes case 2.

Observe that we showed violations of the operational incompressibility game in both cases. This completes the proof.

6.2
Construction and Proofs of Security

In this section, we prove
Theorem

9
: We show that there exists a one-shot signature scheme that satisfies incompressibility (
Definition

21
) relative to a classical oracle. Our construction will be a delicately modified version of the construction of
[
SZ25
]
.

Now we move onto our construction.

Define the following parameters: Let
q(\lambda)=16\lambda,r(\lambda)=q(\lambda)\cdot(\lambda-1),n(\lambda)=r(\lambda)+\frac{3}{2}q(\lambda),k(\lambda)=n(\lambda)
.

\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenOracles}(1^{\lambda})

1.

Sample a random permutation
\pi
on
\{0,1\}^{n}
. Let
H(x),J(x)
denote the first
r
and last
n-r
bits of
\pi(x)
.

2.

For each
y\in\{0,1\}^{r}
, sample a random full column-rank matrix
A_{y}\in\mathds{F}_{2}^{k\times(n-r)}
and a random vector
b_{y}
in
\mathds{F}_{2}^{k}
.

3.

Set
\mathcal{P}(x)=(y,A_{y}\cdot J(x)+b_{y})
where
y=H(x)
.

4.

Set
\mathcal{P}^{-1}(y,v)=\pi^{-1}(y||z)
if
v=A_{y}\cdot z+b_{y}
for some
z\in\mathds{F}_{2}^{n-r}
, otherwise
\bot
.

5.

Set
\mathcal{D}(y,v)=1
if
v^{T}A_{y}=0
and
v\neq 0
, otherwise
0
.

6.

Set
\mathcal{D}_{0}(y,m,v)=1
if
v=A_{y}\cdot z+b_{y}
for some
z\in\mathds{F}_{2}^{n-r}
and the first bit of
v
is
m
; otherwise
0
.

7.

The oracles:
Set
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{Ver}}=\mathcal{D}_{0}
,
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{Sign}}=\mathcal{D}
,
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{GenQKey}}=(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}_{0})
. Set the oracle
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}}
to be
(\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{Ver}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{GenQKey}})
.

\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}^{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{GenQKey}}}(1^{\lambda})

1.

Parse
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{GenQKey}}=(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}_{0})
.

2.

Prepare the states
\ket{0^n},\ket{0^r},\ket{0^k}
over the registers
\mathsf{R}_{1},\mathsf{R}_{2},\mathsf{R}_{3}
.

3.

Apply
H^{\otimes n}
to
\mathsf{R}_{1}
.

4.

Execute the oracle
\mathcal{P}
with input register
\mathsf{R}_{1}
and output register
(\mathsf{R}_{2},\mathsf{R}_{3})
.

5.

Execute the oracle
\mathcal{P}^{-1}
with input register
(\mathsf{R}_{2},\mathsf{R}_{3})
and output register
\mathsf{R}_{1}
.

6.

Measure the register
\mathsf{R}_{2}
, let
y
be the measurement outcome.

7.

Output
y,\mathsf{R}_{3}
.

\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}^{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{Ver}}}(vk,sig,m)

1.

Parse
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{Ver}}=\mathcal{D}_{0}
.

2.

Output
\mathcal{D}_{0}(vk,m,sig)=1
.

Our signing algorithm is the same as the signing algorithm of
[
SZ25
]
(which in turn is the same as the signing mechanism of
[
AGKZ20
,
Shm22
]
). For completeness, we recall it here.

\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}^{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{Sign}}}(\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}},m)

1.

Parse
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{Sign}}=\mathcal{D}
.

2.

Parse
(vk,\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}^{\prime})=\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}

3.

For
j=1,\dots,\lambda
:

1.

Measure the first qubit of
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}^{\prime}
. In case the outcome is
m
, measure the rest of
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}^{\prime}
to obtain
sig^{\prime}
, and output
m||sig^{\prime}
and terminate.

2.

Apply
H^{\otimes k}
to
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}^{\prime}
, then apply the following mapping coherently (writing the outcome to a new output register) to
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}^{\prime}
and measure the output bit:
(vk,z)\to 1
if
\mathcal{D}(vk,z)=1
or if
z=0^{k}
, otherwise
(vk,z)\to 0
. Then, apply
H^{\otimes k}
again to
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}^{\prime}
. Proceed to the next iteration
j\leftarrow j+1
.

4.

If all
\lambda
iterations failed, output
\bot
.

Theorem 11
.

\mathsf{OSS}
satisfies correctness.

Proof.

While the correctness of the scheme follows from previous work (
[
SZ25
]
, which in turn uses the correctness proof of
[
AGKZ20
,
Shm22
]
), for ease of reference, we recall it below. Fix any
m\in\{0,1\}
for the rest of the proof.

First, we assume that for all but (at most)
2\cdot 2^{2r(\lambda)-n(\lambda)}
many values of
y
, we have that
A_{y}
has at least a single column whose first entry is
1
. Since the probability of this happening for any individual
y
value is
1-(1/2)^{n-r}
, by a simple Chernoff bound it is easy to see that our assumption holds with probability at least
1-\exp{-\lambda}
.

It is easy to see that
\mathsf{GenQKey}
outputs
y,\sum_{u\in\mathsf{ColSpan}(A_{y})}\ket{v + b_y}
for a uniformly random
y
, thus we start with this state in the register
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}
. We assume that
A_{y}
has at least a single column whose first entry is
1
. Note that we had already assumed that this is already true for all but (at most)
2\cdot 2^{2r(\lambda)-n(\lambda)}
many verification keys, and since
y
is chosen uniformly at random from
2^{r}
verification keys, our assumption holds with probability at least
1-\exp{-\lambda}
.

Note that by definition signatures for
m
correspond to vectors in
\mathsf{ColSpan}(A_{y})+b_{y}
that start with the bit
m
. Since there is at least one column whose first entry is
1
, it is easy to see that there is an equal number of vectors that start with either bit in
\mathsf{Colspan}(A_{y})+b_{y}
. Thus, the probability of signing in the first iteration of the algorithm is accordingly
1/2
. The algorithm makes
\lambda
tries and we would like to argue it succeeds with probability
1-2^{\lambda}
. We will prove that each try indeed succeeds with at least probability
1/2
(so far it is clear only for the first iteration), which will complete the proof.

Now let
S:=\mathsf{ColSpan}(A_{y})
and let
S_{0}
the set of vectors in
S
that start with
0
, which is a subspace of
S
with one less dimension. Observe that after the failed first try, the state we have in
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}^{\prime}
is the state
\sum_{u\in S_{0}}\ket{b_y + x + u}
, for some value
x\in S
which will not matter for our proof (for the interested reader,
x
will be the zero vector if
b_{y}
starts with the bit
\overline{m}
, and otherwise it will be a vector that
completes

S_{0}
to
S
). Now we claim that that if we have a state of the form
\sum_{u\in S_{0}}(-1)^{\langle z,u\rangle}\ket{b_y + x + u}
, for any
z\in S_{0}^{\bot}
, after performing the correction part of the signing algorithm, we obtain a state of the form
\sum_{u\in S}(-1)^{\langle z^{\prime},u\rangle}\ket{b_y + x + u}
for some
z^{\prime}\in S_{0}^{\bot}
. To see this, note that after executing
H^{\otimes k}
the state we get is
\sum_{v\in S^{\bot}_{0}}(-1)^{\langle b_{y}+x,v\rangle}\ket{z + v}
, and since
S_{0}
is a subspace of
S
with one less dimension, the subspace
S^{\bot}:=\mathsf{ColSpan}(A_{y})^{\bot}
is a subspace of
S^{\bot}_{0}
with one less dimension. Thus, when we measure the output qubit of
\mathcal{D}
(note that we are not using the output of
\mathcal{D}
and effectively discarding its answer) the state collapses to a state of the form
\sum_{v\in S^{\bot}}(-1)^{\langle b_{y}+x,v\rangle}\ket{z' + v}
for some value
z^{\prime}\in S_{0}^{\bot}
. When we execute the second Hadamard, we get a state of the form
\sum_{u\in S}(-1)^{\langle z^{\prime},u\rangle}\ket{b_y + x + u}
, which is equal to
\sum_{u\in S}(-1)^{\langle z^{\prime},u\rangle}\ket{b_y + u}
since
x\in S
. Thus, in summary, as long as we start an iteration with a state of this form, we end the iteration with a state of this form (even the signing attempt fails). With this, it is easy to see that each iteration succeeds with probability
1/2
independently. This finishes the proof.
∎

Theorem 12
.

\mathsf{OSS}
satisfies strong one-shot security.

Proof.

Observe that the oracle
\mathcal{D}_{0}
in our construction can be implemented using
\mathcal{P}^{-1}
. Since strong one-shot security relative to
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D}
has already been proven by
[
SZ25
]
, the security of our scheme follows.
∎

Theorem 13
.

\mathsf{OSS}
satisfies incompressibility.

We prove this theorem in
Section

6.4
.

6.3
Properties of Subspace Oracles

In this section, we recall some results about subspace oracles and also prove some new technical results. Throughout the section, we will write
\mathcal{O}_{S^{\prime}}
to denote the oracle that checks membership in
S^{\prime}
(outputs
1
if and only if the input is inside
S^{\prime}
) where
S^{\prime}
is a subspace.

First we recall the subspace-hiding oracles.

Lemma 2

(Subspace-Hiding Oracles
[
SZ25
]
)
.

Let
k,r,s\in\mathds{N}
be such that
r+s\leq k
and let
S
be a subspace of
\mathds{F}_{2}^{k}
of dimension
r
. Let
\mathcal{U}
be the uniform distribution over superspaces
T
of
S
of dimension
r+s
.

Then, for any adversary
\mathcal{A}
making at most
q
quantum queries, we have the following.

\{\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{O}_{S}}()\}\approx_{O\left(\frac{q\cdot s}{\sqrt{2^{k-r-s}}}\right)}\{\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{O}_{T}}()\;:\;T\leftarrow\mathcal{U}\}\kern 5.0pt.

Now we prove the following theorem, which significantly improves upon a similar result of
[
SZ25
]
.

Theorem 14
.

Let
d_{2}\leq d_{1}\leq n
. Let
S
be a subspace of dimension
d_{1}
of
\mathds{F}_{2}^{n}
, and let
\mathcal{U}
denote the uniform distribution of subspaces of
S
of dimension
d_{2}
. Then, for any adversary
\mathcal{A}
making at most
q
quantum queries, we have

\Pr[v\in T\setminus\{0\}:\begin{array}[]{c}T\leftarrow\mathcal{U}\\
v\leftarrow\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{O}_{T^{\perp}}}\end{array}]\leq\frac{2^{d_{1}-d_{2}}-1}{2^{d_{1}}-1}+q\cdot\sqrt{\frac{2^{d_{1}-d_{2}}-1}{2^{d_{1}}-1}}

Proof.

Let
p_{\mathcal{A}}=\Pr[v\in T\setminus\{0\}:\begin{array}[]{c}T\leftarrow\mathcal{U}\\
v\leftarrow\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{O}_{T^{\perp}}}\end{array}]
. We observe that for any adversary
\mathcal{B}
that is not given query access to
\mathcal{O}_{T^{\perp}}
(call such an adversary an
oblivious adversary
), the probability of outputting a vector
v\in T^{\perp}\setminus S^{\perp}
is
p=\frac{2^{d_{1}-d_{2}}-1}{2^{d_{1}}-1}
. Thus, by
Theorem

6
, if we modify the first oracle call of
\mathcal{A}
to instead use
\mathcal{O}_{S^{\perp}}
, the probability of winning decreases by at most
\sqrt{p}
, since the part of the adversary before its first call to
\mathcal{O}_{T^{\perp}}
can be considered an oblivious adversary. Further, once we modify the first oracle call of the adversary to
\mathcal{O}_{S^{\perp}}
, now the part until the second oracle call is also an oblivious adversary. Continuing this argument for all
q
oracle calls, we can conclude that when
\mathcal{A}
is run with the oracle
\mathcal{O}_{S^{\perp}}
, its probability of winning is at least
p_{\mathcal{A}}-q\cdot\sqrt{p}
. However, by the same oblivious algorithm argument as above, we have
p_{\mathcal{A}}-q\cdot\sqrt{p}\leq p
. Thus,
p_{\mathcal{A}}\leq q\cdot\sqrt{p}+p
.

∎

6.4
Proof of Incompressibility

In this section, we prove
Theorem

13
. In the proof, instead of setting
s(\lambda)=(q(\lambda))^{c}
for some
c>0
, we actually set
s(\lambda)=2q(\lambda)
, which is even stronger.

Now, we move onto our proof. We will explicitly prove only the case of
\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}}
, and the case of
\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}}
follows essentially the same way (note the symmetry between
A_{y}
and
A_{y}^{\perp}
in our proof).

Suppose for a contradiction that incompressibility is not satisfied: There exists query-bounded
\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2}
and a polynomial
p(\cdot)
such that
p_{win}\geq\frac{1}{p(\lambda)}
, where we define

\displaystyle p_{win}=\Pr[\begin{array}[]{c}\forall i\in[s(\lambda)+1]~\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{Ver}}(vk_{i},m_{i},sig_{i})\neq 0\\
\wedge\\
{(vk_{i})}_{i\in[s(\lambda)+1]}~\text{distinct}\end{array}:\begin{array}[]{c}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}})\leftarrow\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenOracles}(1^{\lambda})\\
L\leftarrow\mathcal{P}_{1}^{(\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}})}(1^{\lambda})\\
(vk_{i},m_{i},sig_{i})_{i\in[s(\lambda)+1]}\leftarrow\mathcal{P}_{2}^{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}}}(L)\end{array}]

where
s(\lambda)=2q(\lambda)
and
q(\lambda)
is the number of queries to
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}}
made by
\mathcal{P}_{1}
.

Now define a sequence of hybrids for some adversaries
\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2}
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
:

We define this hybrid as follows.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}

1.

Sample a random permutation
\pi
on
\{0,1\}^{n}
.

2.

For each
y\in\{0,1\}^{r}
, sample a random full column-rank matrix
A_{y}\in\mathds{F}_{2}^{k\times(n-r)}
and a random vector
b_{y}
in
\mathds{F}_{2}^{k}
.

3.

Set
\mathcal{P}(x)=(y,{A_{{y}}}\cdot J(x)+b_{y})
where
y=H(x)
.

4.

Set
\mathcal{P}^{-1}(y,v)=\pi^{-1}(y||z)
if
v={A_{{y}}}\cdot z+b_{y}
for some
z\in\mathds{F}_{2}^{n-r}
, otherwise
\bot
.

5.

Set
\mathcal{D}(y,v)=1
if
v^{T}{A_{{y}}}=0
and
v\neq 0
, otherwise
0
.

6.

Set
\mathcal{D}_{0}(y,m,v)=1
if
v={A_{{y}}}\cdot z+b_{y}
for some
z\in\mathds{F}_{2}^{n-r}
and the first bit of
v
is
m
; otherwise
0
.

7.

Set
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{Ver}}=\mathcal{D}_{0}
,
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{Sign}}=\mathcal{D}
,
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{GenQKey}}=(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}_{0})
.

8.

Sample
L\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{1}^{(\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}})}(1^{\lambda})
.

9.

Sample
(vk_{i},m_{i},v_{i})_{i\in[q(\lambda)+1]}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}}}(L,(b_{y})_{y\in\{0,1\}^{r}})
.

10.

Check that
vk_{i}
are all distinct, and that
v_{i}+b_{vk_{i}}
starts with the bit
m_{i}
and that
v_{i}\in\mathsf{Colspan}(A_{vk_{i}})\setminus\{0\}
for all
i\in[q+1]
. If so, output
1
, otherwise output
0
.

We set
\mathcal{A}_{1}
to
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
to be the following adversary. It first runs
\mathcal{A}_{1}
to obtain
(vk_{i},m_{i},sig_{i})_{i\in[s(\lambda)+1]}
, then it checks if there are at least
q+1
values of
{i\in[s(\lambda)+1]}
such that
sig_{i}\neq b_{vk_{i}}
. If so, let
I\subset[s(\lambda)+1]
(with
|I|=q+1
) denote those values and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
outputs
(vk_{i},m_{i},sig_{i}-b_{vk_{i}})_{i\in I}
. Otherwise, it outputs
\bot
.

Then, we claim that we have
\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]\geq\frac{1}{2p}
. To see this, first define the event
E_{1}
to be the event in the definition of
p_{win}
. Define
E_{2}
to be the event that for at least
q+1
values of
i\in[s+1]
, we have
sig_{i}\neq b_{vk_{i}}
(where the same sampling procedure is the same as in the definition of
p_{win}
). Now, observe that we have
\Pr[E_{1}\wedge E_{2}]+\Pr[E_{1}\wedge\overline{E_{2}}]=p_{win}\geq\frac{1}{p}
. Now we claim that
\Pr[E_{1}\wedge\overline{E_{2}}]\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
. Observe that once we prove this,
\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]\geq\frac{1}{2p}
follows easily for
\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2}
we chose above.

Now we prove
\Pr[E_{1}\wedge\overline{E_{2}}]\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
. Observe that once
E_{1}\wedge\overline{E_{2}}
occurs, it means that we have recovered the value
b_{vk}
as part of the output
\mathcal{P}_{2}
for at least
q+1
different
vk
values. Also note that each query to
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}}
made by
\mathcal{P}_{2}
can be simulated by making
2^{n-r}
queries to an oracle that verifies equality for
b_{y}
values (i.e. the oracle that outputs
1
on
b_{y},y
and
0
otherwise). Thus,
\mathcal{P}_{1}
makes
q
queries to the oracle
\mathcal{O}(y)=b_{y}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
makes
\mathsf{poly}(\lambda)\cdot 2^{n-r}
queries to the verification/equality check oracle for
b_{y}
values. Thus, by
Theorem

15
and by the values of the parameters
n,r,k
, we get that (except with negligibly small probability)
\mathcal{P}_{2}
cannot output
b_{vk}
values for
q+1
different
vk
values. Hence,
\Pr[E_{1}\wedge\overline{E_{2}}]\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
as desired.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
:

Let
t(\lambda)
denote the total number of queries in the experiment. Set
\ell(\lambda)=1024\cdot C_{\mathsf{SRD}}\cdot p(\lambda)\cdot(t(\lambda))^{3}
where
C_{\mathsf{SRD}}
is the constant from
Theorem

8
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}

1.

Sample a random permutation
\pi
on
\{0,1\}^{n}
.

2.

For each
y\in\{0,1\}^{r}
, sample a random full column-rank matrix
A_{y}\in\mathds{F}_{2}^{k\times(n-r)}
and a random vector
b_{y}
in
\mathds{F}_{2}^{k}
.

3.

Sample a random function
SRD:\{0,1\}^{r}\to[\ell]
.

4.

Set
\mathcal{P}(x)=(y,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}A_{{SRD(y)}}}\cdot J(x)+b_{y})
where
y=H(x)
.

5.

Set
\mathcal{P}^{-1}(y,v)=\pi^{-1}(y||z)
if
v={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}A_{{SRD(y)}}}\cdot z+b_{y}
for some
z\in\mathds{F}_{2}^{n-r}
, otherwise
\bot
.

6.

Set
\mathcal{D}(y,v)=1
if
v^{T}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}A_{{SRD(y)}}}=0
and
v\neq 0
, otherwise
0
.

7.

Set
\mathcal{D}_{0}(y,m,v)=1
if
v={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}A_{{SRD(y)}}}\cdot z+b_{y}
for some
z\in\mathds{F}_{2}^{n-r}
and the first bit of
v
is
m
; otherwise
0
.

8.

Set
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{Ver}}=\mathcal{D}_{0}
,
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{Sign}}=\mathcal{D}
,
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{GenQKey}}=(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}_{0})
.

9.

Sample
L\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{1}^{(\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}})}(1^{\lambda})
.

10.

Sample
(vk_{i},m_{i},v_{i})_{i\in[q(\lambda)+1]}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}}}(L,(b_{y})_{y\in\{0,1\}^{r}})
.

11.

Check that
vk_{i}
are all distinct, and that
v_{i}+b_{vk_{i}}
starts with the bit
m_{i}
and that
v_{i}\in\mathsf{Colspan}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}A_{SRD(vk_{i})}})\setminus\{0\}
for all
i\in[q+1]
. If so, output
1
, otherwise output
0
.

We have
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}\approx_{1/16p}\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
by
Theorem

8
, since the whole experiment can be implemented with oracle access to the oracle
\mathcal{O}_{A}(y)=A_{y}
(where each
\mathcal{O}_{A}(y)
is sampled independently from the full-column-rank matrix distribution as above)

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
:

We define this hybrid as follows.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}

1.

Sample a random permutation
\pi
on
\{0,1\}^{n}
.

2.

For each
y\in\{0,1\}^{r}
, sample a random full column-rank matrix
A_{y}\in\mathds{F}_{2}^{k\times(n-r)}
and a random vector
b_{y}
in
\mathds{F}_{2}^{k}
.

3.

For each
y\in\{0,1\}^{r}
, sample
A^{\prime}_{y}\in\mathds{F}_{2}^{k\times(n-\frac{r}{2})}
and
A^{\prime\prime}_{y}\in\mathds{F}_{2}^{k\times(n-\frac{r}{2})}
so that
\mathsf{Colspan}(A^{\prime}_{y})
is a random superspace of
\mathsf{Colspan}(A_{y})
of dimension
n-\frac{r}{2}
and
\mathsf{Colspan}(A^{\prime\prime}_{y})^{\perp}
is a random superspace of
\mathsf{Colspan}(A_{y})^{\perp}
of dimension
\frac{3r}{2}
.

4.

Sample a random function
SRD:\{0,1\}^{r}\to[\ell]
.

5.

Set
\mathcal{P}(x)=(y,{A_{{SRD(y)}}}\cdot J(x)+b_{y})
where
y=H(x)
.

6.

Set
\mathcal{P}^{-1}(y,v)=\pi^{-1}(y||z)
if
v={A_{{SRD(y)}}}\cdot z+b_{y}
for some
z\in\mathds{F}_{2}^{n-r}
, otherwise
\bot
.

7.

Set
\mathcal{D}(y,v)=1
if
v^{T}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}A^{\prime\prime}_{{SRD(y)}}}=0
and
v\neq 0
, otherwise
0
.

8.

Set
\mathcal{D}_{0}(y,m,v)=1
if
v={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}A^{\prime}_{{SRD(y)}}}\cdot z+b_{y}
for some
z\in\mathds{F}_{2}^{n-r}
and the first bit of
v
is
m
; otherwise
0
.

9.

Set
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{Ver}}=\mathcal{D}_{0}
,
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{Sign}}=\mathcal{D}
,
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{GenQKey}}=(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}_{0})
.

10.

Sample
L\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{1}^{(\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}})}(1^{\lambda})
.

11.

Sample
(vk_{i},m_{i},v_{i})_{i\in[q(\lambda)+1]}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}}}(L,(b_{y})_{y\in\{0,1\}^{r}})
.

12.

Check that
vk_{i}
are all distinct, and that
v_{i}+b_{vk_{i}}
starts with the bit
m_{i}
and that
v_{i}\in\mathsf{Colspan}({A_{SRD(vk_{i})}})\setminus{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{Colspan}({A^{\prime\prime}_{SRD(vk_{i})}})}
for all
i\in[q+1]
. If so, output
1
, otherwise output
0
.

We have
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}\approx\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
by the following argument. First, note that the changes to the oracles
\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}_{0}
make negligible difference by
Lemma

2
(since we only deal with
\ell
, which is polynomial, many subspaces). Also note that
Lemma

2
allows the adversary to choose the base subspace, thus, the oracles
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1}
and so on do not preclude us from invoking the theorem. After we modify the oracles, the change to the winning condition also makes negligible difference by
Theorem

14
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
:

We define this hybrid as follows.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}

1.

Sample a random permutation
\pi
on
\{0,1\}^{n}
.

2.

For each
y\in\{0,1\}^{r}
, sample a random full column-rank matrix
A_{y}\in\mathds{F}_{2}^{k\times(n-r)}
and a random vector
b_{y}
in
\mathds{F}_{2}^{k}
.

3.

For each
y\in\{0,1\}^{r}
, sample
A^{\prime}_{y}\in\mathds{F}_{2}^{k\times(n-\frac{r}{2})}
and
A^{\prime\prime}_{y}\in\mathds{F}_{2}^{k\times(n-\frac{r}{2})}
so that
\mathsf{Colspan}(A^{\prime}_{y})
is a random superspace of
\mathsf{Colspan}(A_{y})
of dimension
n-\frac{r}{2}
and
\mathsf{Colspan}(A^{\prime\prime}_{y})^{\perp}
is a random superspace of
\mathsf{Colspan}(A_{y})^{\perp}
of dimension
\frac{3r}{2}
(or, equivalently,
\mathsf{Colspan}(A^{\prime\prime}_{y})
is a random subspace of
\mathsf{Colspan}(A_{y})
of dimension
n-\frac{3r}{2}
.)

4.

Set
\mathcal{P}(x)=(y,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}A_{y}}\cdot J(x)+b_{y})
where
y=H(x)
.

5.

Set
\mathcal{P}^{-1}(y,v)=\pi^{-1}(y||z)
if
v={{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}A_{y}}}\cdot z+b_{y}
for some
z\in\mathds{F}_{2}^{n-r}
, otherwise
\bot
.

6.

Set
\mathcal{D}(y,v)=1
if
v^{T}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}A^{\prime\prime}_{y}}=0
and
v\neq 0
, otherwise
0
.

7.

Set
\mathcal{D}_{0}(y,m,v)=1
if
v={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}A^{\prime}_{y}}\cdot z+b_{y}
for some
z\in\mathds{F}_{2}^{n-r}
and the first bit of
v
is
m
; otherwise
0
.

8.

Set
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{Ver}}=\mathcal{D}_{0}
,
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{Sign}}=\mathcal{D}
,
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{GenQKey}}=(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}^{-1},\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}_{0})
.

9.

Sample
L\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{1}^{(\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}})}(1^{\lambda})
.

10.

Sample
(vk_{i},m_{i},v_{i})_{i\in[q(\lambda)+1]}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}}}(L,(b_{y})_{y\in\{0,1\}^{r}})
.

11.

Check that
vk_{i}
are all distinct, and that
v_{i}+b_{vk_{i}}
starts with the bit
m_{i}
and that
v_{i}\in{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{Colspan}({A_{vk_{i}}})\setminus\mathsf{Colspan}({A^{\prime\prime}_{vk_{i}}})}
for all
i\in[q+1]
. If so, output
1
, otherwise output
0
.

We have
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}\approx_{1/16p}\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
by
Theorem

8
, since the whole experiment can be implemented with oracle access to the oracle
\mathcal{O}_{A}(y)=A_{y}^{\prime\prime}||A_{y}||A_{y}^{\prime}
(where each
\mathcal{O}_{A}(y)
is sampled independently as in the experiment).

\mathsf{Hyb}_{final}
:

We now define our final hybrid, for some adversaries
\mathcal{A}_{1}^{\prime},\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\prime}
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{final}

1.

Sample a random permutation
\pi
on
\{0,1\}^{n}
.

2.

For each
y\in\{0,1\}^{r}
, sample a random vector
b_{y}
in
\mathds{F}_{2}^{k}
.

3.

For each
y\in\{0,1\}^{r}
, sample
A^{\prime}_{y}\in\mathds{F}_{2}^{k\times(n-\frac{r}{2})}
and
A^{\prime\prime}_{y}\in\mathds{F}_{2}^{k\times(n-\frac{r}{2})}
so that
\mathsf{Colspan}(A^{\prime}_{y})
is a random subspace of
\mathds{F}_{2}^{k}
of dimension
n-\frac{r}{2}
and so that
\mathsf{Colspan}(A^{\prime\prime}_{y})
is a random subspace of
\mathsf{Colspan}(A^{\prime}_{y})
of dimension
n-\frac{3r}{2}
.

4.

For each
y\in\{0,1\}^{r}
, sample
A_{y}\in\mathds{F}_{2}^{k\times(n-r)}
so that
\mathsf{Colspan}(A_{y})
is a random subspace from the set
\mathcal{S}
of subspaces of dimension
n-r
where each subspace in the set is a superspace of
\mathsf{Colspan}(A^{\prime\prime}_{y})
and subspace of
\mathsf{Colspan}(A^{\prime}_{y})
.

5.

Define the oracle
\mathcal{O}(y)
that outputs the index of
\mathsf{Colspan}(A_{y})
in the set
\mathcal{S}
when it is ordered in a canonical way. That is, each
\mathcal{O}(y)
is independently and uniformly sampled from
[|\mathcal{S}|]
(we will refer to the elements of
[|\mathcal{S}|]
and the subspaces
A_{y}
in an interchangeable way).

6.

Sample
L\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{1}^{\mathcal{O}}(1^{\lambda},\pi,(b_{y},A^{\prime}_{y},A^{\prime\prime}_{y})_{y\in\{0,1\}^{r}})
.

7.

Sample
(vk_{i},m_{i},v_{i})_{i\in[q(\lambda)+1]}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{2}(L,\pi,(b_{y},A^{\prime}_{y},A^{\prime\prime}_{y})_{y\in\{0,1\}^{r}})
.

8.

Winning Condition Check:
Check that
vk_{i}
are all distinct, and that
v_{i}+b_{vk_{i}}
starts with the bit
m_{i}
and that
v_{i}\in{\mathsf{Colspan}({A_{vk_{i}}})\setminus\mathsf{Colspan}({A^{\prime\prime}_{vk_{i}}})}
for all
i\in[q+1]
. If so, output
1
, otherwise output
0
.

Since
\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}=1]\geq\frac{1}{2p}
, it is easy to see that there exists
\mathcal{A}_{1}^{\prime},\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\prime}
such that
\mathcal{A}_{1}^{\prime}
makes
q
queries to
\mathcal{O}
and
\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{final}=1]\geq\frac{1}{2p}
. Now we will show that this is a contradiction. Moving forward, without loss of generality, we will assume that
\mathcal{A}_{2}
never outputs (i)
v_{i}\not\in{\mathsf{Colspan}({A^{\prime}_{vk_{i}}})\setminus\mathsf{Colspan}({A^{\prime\prime}_{vk_{i}}})}
, (ii) it never outputs non-distinct
vk_{i}
values and (iii) it never outputs
\bot
(e.g. at worst, it can output random vectors in
{\mathsf{Colspan}({A^{\prime}_{vk_{i}}})\setminus\mathsf{Colspan}({A^{\prime\prime}_{vk_{i}}})}
). Now, first, consider the compressed oracle simulation,
\mathsf{Exp}
of the hybrid
\mathsf{Hyb}_{final}
. In particular, we also execute the final part of the experiment (i.e. winning condition check) using queries to
\mathcal{O}
: For
i\in[q+1]
, we first query the oracle
\mathcal{O}
on
vk_{i}
and write the output to an empty register, and then we perform the membership check for
v_{i}
by reading from this register. We assume that the output (win or lose bit) is also written to a new register (call it the experiment outcome register). Since compressed oracle simulation is a perfect simulation, we have
\Pr[\mathsf{Exp}=1]=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{final}=1]
. Now we define the experiment
\mathsf{Exp}^{\prime}
where we modify the execution of the compressed oracle simulation during the winning condition check as follows. Instead of applying
\mathsf{StdDecomp}\circ\mathsf{CStO}^{\prime}\circ\mathsf{StdDecomp}
(we assume that
\mathsf{Increase}
has been applied for sufficiently many times in advance), we instead apply
\mathsf{StdDecomp}\circ\mathsf{CStO}^{\prime}\circ\mathsf{StdDecomp}^{\prime}
where
\mathsf{StdDecomp}^{\prime}
is a linear mapping defined as
\mathsf{StdDecomp}^{\prime}:\ket{vk}\ket{v}\ket{D}\to\ket{vk}\ket{v}\sum_{A\in[|S|]:v\not\in{\mathsf{Colspan}(A)\setminus\mathsf{Colspan}({A^{\prime\prime}_{vk}})}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{a}}\ket{D \cup(vk, A)}
for
D,vk
such that
D(vk)=\bot
(and defined the same as
\mathsf{StdDecomp}
for other basis elements), where
a=\begin{bmatrix}r\\
r/2\end{bmatrix}_{2}-\begin{bmatrix}r-1\\
r/2-1\end{bmatrix}_{2}
. We will call the case of
D,vk
such that
D(vk)=\bot
a
good case
. Since the original mapping
\mathsf{StdDecomp}
maps
\ket{vk}\ket{v}\ket{D}\to\ket{vk}\ket{v}\sum_{A\in[|S|]}\frac{1}{\sqrt{|S|}}\ket{D \cup(vk, A)}
for
D
such that
D(vk)=\bot
, and since
|S|=\begin{bmatrix}r\\
r/2\end{bmatrix}_{2}
, it is easy to see that
\mathsf{Exp}^{\prime}\approx\mathsf{Exp}
. Finally, observe that
\Pr[\mathsf{Exp}^{\prime}=1]=0
: For any output
(vk_{i},m_{i},v_{i})_{i\in[q(\lambda)+1]}
(of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
) and any compressed oracle database
D
that is in the support of the system when
\mathcal{A}_{2}
yields its output, it will be the case that there is at least one value
i\in[q+1]
such that
vk_{i}\not\in D
since
D
contains
q
valid pairs (also recall that when the good case does not apply, the number of valid pairs in the database does not increase). Thus, while applying the winning condition check, there will be a point where the good case of
\mathsf{StdDecomp}^{\prime}
applies, and after that it is easy to see that verification for that index fails. Thus, any
(vk_{i},m_{i},v_{i})_{i\in[q(\lambda)+1]}
and any compressed oracle database
D
in the support of the system will map to a state where the experiment outcome register will have the state
\ket{0}
. Thus,
\Pr[\mathsf{Exp}^{\prime}=1]=0
. However, we had
\mathsf{Exp}^{\prime}\approx\mathsf{Exp}
and
\Pr[\mathsf{Exp}=1]=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{final}=1]
and
\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{final}=1]\geq\frac{1}{2p}
. Thus, this is a contradiction, as desired.

7
Incompressibility of Random Oracles

In this section, we show that random oracles satisfy a natural notion of incompressibility. First we give our theorem statement regarding this property, then we prove a technical lemma that will help us in our proof of the incompressibility theorem, and finally we prove our incompressibility theorem.

Theorem 15

(Incompressibility of Random Oracles)
.

Let
H:\{0,1\}^{g_{1}(\lambda)}\to\{0,1\}^{g_{2}(\lambda)}
be a random oracle and define
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{ImageVer}}
to be the following oracle (called
(image) verification oracle
).

\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{ImageVer}}(x,y)

Hardcoded:
H

1.

Check if
y=H(x)
. If so, output
1
. Otherwise, output
0
.

For a predicate
P:\{0,1\}^{g_{1}(\lambda)}\to\{0,1\}
, define
\mathcal{O}_{P}
as follows.

\mathcal{O}(x)

Hardcoded:
H,P

1.

Check if
P(x)=0
. If so, output
\bot
and terminate.

2.

Output
H(x)
.

Define the following experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{H}
.

\mathsf{Exp}_{H}

1.

Set
i=1
.

2.

For any polynomial number of rounds (denote this by
u(\lambda)
):

1.

\mathcal{A}_{1}
makes polynomial number of queries (denote the number by
s_{i}(\lambda)
) to
H
and unbounded number of queries to
\mathcal{O}_{\cup^{i-1}_{j=1}P_{j}}
where
{\cup^{i-1}_{j=1}P_{j}}
denotes the union of the predicates
P_{1},\dots,P_{i-1}
(i.e.
{\cup^{i-1}_{j=1}P_{j}}(x)=1
if
P_{j}(x)=1
for some
j=1,\dots,i-1
).

2.

\mathcal{A}_{1}
outputs a predicate
P_{i}
.

3.

Increase
i
by one.

3.

\mathcal{A}
outputs an unbounded length message
L
.

For any
g_{1}(\lambda),g_{2}(\lambda)
such that
g_{2}(\lambda)
is superlogarithmic, for an adversary
\mathcal{A}_{1}
as described above,
there exists a mapping
\mathcal{M}
(which outputs a list
22
22

22

We will refer to this as the incompressibility list.

\mathfrak{L}
of size at most
\sum_{j\in[u(\lambda)]}s_{j}(\lambda)
) such that for all
\mathcal{A}_{2}
that makes at most
q(\lambda)
queries to
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{ImageVer}}
such that
\frac{q(\lambda)}{\sqrt{2^{g_{2}(\lambda)}}}
is negligible (but can make any number of queries to
\mathcal{O}_{P^{*}}
), we have

\mathds{E}\left[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y:\\
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{ImageVer}}(x,y)\neq 0\\
x\not\in\mathcal{M}(H,L,(P_{j})_{j},\mathcal{A}_{2})\\
P^{*}(x)=0\end{subarray}}\mathsf{QW}^{x,y}_{\mathcal{A}_{2},L,H,(P_{j})_{j}}:\begin{array}[]{c}H\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{g_{1}(\lambda)}\to\{0,1\}^{g_{2}(\lambda)}\\
L,(P_{j})_{j\in[u(\lambda)]}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{1}^{H(\cdot)}(1^{\lambda})\end{array}\right]\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

where
P^{*}={\cup_{j\in[u(\lambda)]}P_{j}}
and
\mathsf{QW}^{x,y}_{\mathcal{A}_{2},L,H,(P_{j})_{j}}
denotes the total query weight of
\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\mathcal{O}_{P^{*}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{ImageVer}}}(L)
to the oracle
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{ImageVer}}
on oracle input
x,y
.

We will refer to the queries to
\mathcal{O}_{P^{*}}
(for
\mathcal{A}_{2}
) and
\mathcal{O}_{\cup^{i-1}_{j=1}P_{j}}
(for
\mathcal{A}_{1}
) as
section queries
(and to these oracles as
section oracles
), and queries to
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{ImageVer}}
as verification queries. A normal query will mean a query to
H
.

Before proving our theorem, we will prove a different technical lemma that will be useful in our proof. This lemma roughly says that, for a compressed random oracle adversary with additional verification oracle access to the random oracle; if at any point, the weight of databases that contain at most
i
inputs that satisfy a certain predicate is negligible, then this still holds after a verification query.

Lemma 3
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an adversary with access to a random oracle
H:\{0,1\}^{m(\lambda)}\to\{0,1\}^{n(\lambda)}
and the verification oracle for the random oracle that outputs
1
on input
x,y
if
H(x)=y
, and outputs
0
otherwise. Define the registers

•

\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y}
, the input registers to the verification oracle,

•

\mathsf{Q}
, the 1-qubit phase register for the verification oracle output,

•

\mathsf{W}
, the internal workspace register of the adversary,

•

\mathsf{P}
, the register containing a predicate
23
23

23

We will write
P
to both mean the predicate function, and to mean the set of inputs
x
such that
P(x)=1
. Similarly,
\overline{P}
means the complement of the set
P
, chosen by the adversary,

•

\mathsf{D}
compressed oracle database register,

•

\mathsf{T}
, the register used for implementing the verification oracle queries (initialized at the beginning to zeroes and is not accessed by the adversary).

Consider the following simulation of execution of
\mathcal{A}
:

•

The random oracle queries are implemented as a standard oracle (i.e. the output XORed into the output register) using a compressed oracle simulation,

•

The verification oracle queries are implemented as a phase oracle as follows:

1.

Query the random oracle with input and output registers
X
and the output register
T
.

2.

Apply a controlled phase gate that maps
\ket{x}\ket{y}\ket{q}\ket{t}
to
(-1)^{b\cdot q}\ket{x}\ket{y}\ket{q}\ket{t}
where
b=1
if and only if
y=t
(and otherwise
b=0
).

3.

Query the random oracle with input and output registers
X
and the output register
T
.

Then,

•

This is a perfect simulation of execution of
\mathcal{A}
,

•

Let
\ket{\phi}=\sum_{x,y,q,w,P,D}\alpha_{x,y,q,w,P,D}\ket{x}\ket{y}\ket{q}\ket{w}\ket{P}\ket{D}\ket{0^{n(\lambda)}}
be the state of the system right before executing one of the verification oracle queries, respectively over the registers
\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y},\mathsf{Q},\mathsf{W},\mathsf{P},\mathsf{D},\mathsf{T}
. Let
i\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\mathsf{Inp}(D)
denote the set consisting of all
a
such that
(a,b_{a})\in D
for some
b_{a}\neq\perp
. Let let
\ket{\psi}=\sum_{x,y,q,w,P,D}\beta_{x,y,q,w,P,D}\ket{x}\allowbreak\ket{y}\ket{q}\allowbreak\ket{w}\allowbreak\ket{P}\ket{D}\ket{0^{n(\lambda)}}
be the state right after executing the verification oracle query on
\ket{\phi}
.

Then,
\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y,q,w,P,D:\\
|\mathsf{Inp}(D)\cap\overline{P}|>i\end{subarray}}|\beta_{x,y,q,w,P,D}|^{2}\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y,q,w,P,D:\\
|\mathsf{Inp}(D)\cap\overline{P}|>i\end{subarray}}|\alpha_{x,y,q,w,P,D}|^{2}+\frac{6}{\sqrt{2^{n(\lambda)}}}
.

Proof.

The fact this simulation is a perfect simulation follows easily from the fact that compressed oracle simulation is a perfect simulation for the random oracle (
[
Zha19
]
). Now we prove the second part.
Let
\mathsf{CtPh}
denote the unitary for the controlled phase described above that is used in the implementation of the verification queries. Let
U=\mathsf{CStO}\circ\mathsf{CtPh}\circ\mathsf{CStO}
(where
\mathsf{CStO}
is the compressed oracle query unitary with input register
X
and output register
T
). Note that
U
is an isometry. Thus,
\ket{\psi}=U\ket{\phi}
(here our notation means applying the isometry
U
to the state
\ket{\phi}
). We also let
\pi
denote
24
24

24

By abuse of notation, we will also write
\pi
to denote the measurement
\pi\otimes I
where the identity
I
is over the remaining registers.
the projection onto
\ket{P}\ket{D}
such that
|\mathsf{Inp}(D)\cap\overline{P}|>i
. Thus, we have
\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y,q,w,P,D:\\
|\mathsf{Inp}(D)\cap\overline{P}|>i\end{subarray}}|\beta_{x,y,q,w,P,D}|^{2}=\norm{\pi U\ket{\phi}}^{2}
.

We write

\displaystyle\ket{\phi}=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y,q,w,P,D:\\
|\mathsf{Inp}(D)\cap\overline{P}|>i\end{subarray}}\alpha_{x,y,q,w,P,D}\ket{x}\ket{y}\ket{q}\ket{w}\ket{P}\ket{D}\ket{0^{n(\lambda)}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y,q,w,P,D:\\
|\mathsf{Inp}(D)\cap\overline{P}|\leq i\\
x\in D\end{subarray}}\alpha_{x,y,w,P,D}\ket{x}\ket{y}\ket{q}\ket{w}\ket{P}\ket{D}\ket{0^{n(\lambda)}}+

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y,q,w,P,D:\\
|\mathsf{Inp}(D)\cap\overline{P}|\leq i\\
x\not\in D\\
P(x)=1\end{subarray}}\alpha_{x,y,w,P,D}\ket{x}\ket{y}\ket{q}\ket{w}\ket{P}\ket{D}\ket{0^{n(\lambda)}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y,q,w,P,D:\\
|\mathsf{Inp}(D)\cap\overline{P}|\leq i\\
x\not\in D\\
P(x)=0\end{subarray}}\alpha_{x,y,q,w,P,D}\ket{x}\ket{y}\ket{q}\ket{w}\ket{P}\ket{D}\ket{0^{n(\lambda)}}

Let
\ket{\phi_1},\ket{\phi_2},\ket{\phi_3},\ket{\phi_4}
denote each unnormalized state in the summation above in the order they are written. Observe that when executing a verification query on
\ket{x}\ket{y}\ket{q}\ket{w}\ket{P}\ket{D}
, only
x
can be added to the database (if not already in the database), and the other entries in the database do not change. Thus, we get
\pi U\ket{\phi_2}=0
. By the same argument, we get
\pi U\ket{\phi_3}=0
. We also have
