\norm{\pi U \ket{\phi_1}}^{2}\leq\norm{\ket{\phi_1}}^{2}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y,q,w,P,D:\\
|\mathsf{Inp}(D)\cap\overline{P}|>i\end{subarray}}|\alpha_{x,y,q,w,P,D}|^{2}
.

Now we claim
\norm{\pi U \ket{\phi_4}}^{2}\leq\frac{4}{{2^{n}}}
will prove our claim. Observe that once we prove this, the theorem follows. To see this, first observe that
\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y,q,w,P,D:\\
|\mathsf{Inp}(D)\cap\overline{P}|>i\end{subarray}}|\beta_{x,y,q,w,P,D}|^{2}=\norm{\pi U\ket{\phi}}^{2}\leq\norm{\pi U \ket{\phi_1}}^{2}+\norm{\pi U \ket{\phi_4}}^{2}+2\cdot\norm{\pi U \ket{\phi_1}}\cdot\norm{\pi U \ket{\phi_4}}
by triangle inequality and since
\pi U\ket{\phi_2}=\pi U\ket{\phi_3}=0
. Finally, note that
\norm{\pi U \ket{\phi_1}}^{2}+\norm{\pi U \ket{\phi_4}}^{2}+2\cdot\norm{\pi U \ket{\phi_1}}\cdot\norm{\pi U \ket{\phi_4}}=\norm{\pi U \ket{\phi_1}}^{2}+\norm{\pi U \ket{\phi_4}}(\norm{\pi U \ket{\phi_4}}+2\norm{\pi U \ket{\phi_1}})
and
(\norm{\pi U \ket{\phi_4}}+2\norm{\pi U \ket{\phi_1}})\leq 3
.

Now we move onto proving
\norm{\pi U \ket{\phi_4}}^{2}\leq\frac{4}{{2^{n}}}
.
For all valid states (i.e. states where compressed oracle invariants are kept)
\ket{\tau}
, we have
U\ket{\tau}=\mathsf{StdDecomp}\circ\mathsf{CStO}^{\prime}\circ\mathsf{CPh}\circ\mathsf{CStO}^{\prime}\circ\mathsf{StdDecomp}\circ(\mathsf{Inc})^{2}\ket{\tau}
. This follows from the fact that unitaries on different registers commute, that
\mathsf{Increase}
can be moved to the beginning, and that
\mathsf{StdDecomp}
is an involution. Write
U_{1}=\mathsf{CStO}^{\prime}\circ\mathsf{StdDecomp}\circ(\mathsf{Inc})^{2}
and
U_{3}=\mathsf{StdDecomp}\circ\mathsf{CStO}^{\prime}
; and thus
U=U_{3}\circ\mathsf{CPh}\circ U_{1}
.

After applying
U_{1}
to
\ket{\phi_4}
, the state becomes

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y,q,w,P,D,z:\\
z\in\{0,1\}^{n(\lambda)}\\
|\mathsf{Inp}(D)\cap\overline{P}|\leq i\\
x\not\in D\\
P(x)=0\end{subarray}}\alpha_{x,y,q,w,P,D}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{n(\lambda)}}}\ket{x}\ket{y}\ket{q}\ket{w}\ket{P}\ket{D \cup(x, z) \cup(\bot, 0^n)}\ket{z}

Once we apply the controlled phase based on the verification, the state becomes

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y,q,w,P,D:\\
|\mathsf{Inp}(D)\cap\overline{P}|\leq i\\
x\not\in D\\
P(x)=0\end{subarray}}(-1)^{q}\alpha_{x,y,q,w,P,D}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{n(\lambda)}}}\ket{x}\ket{y}\ket{q}\ket{w}\ket{P}\ket{D \cup(x, y) \cup(\bot, 0^n)}\ket{y}+

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y,q,w,P,D:\\
|\mathsf{Inp}(D)\cap\overline{P}|\leq i\\
x\not\in D\\
P(x)=0\end{subarray}}\sum_{z\neq y}\alpha_{x,y,q,w,P,D}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{n(\lambda)}}}\ket{x}\ket{y}\ket{q}\ket{w}\ket{P}\ket{D \cup(x, z) \cup(\bot, 0^n)}\ket{z}

which is equal to

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y,q,w,P,D:\\
|\mathsf{Inp}(D)\cap\overline{P}|\leq i\\
x\not\in D\\
P(x)=0\end{subarray}}((-1)^{q}-1)\alpha_{x,y,q,w,P,D}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{n(\lambda)}}}\ket{x}\ket{y}\ket{q}\ket{w}\ket{P}\ket{D \cup(x, y) \cup(\bot, 0^n)}\ket{y}+

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y,q,w,P,D:\\
|\mathsf{Inp}(D)\cap\overline{P}|\leq i\\
x\not\in D\\
P(x)=0\end{subarray}}\sum_{z}\alpha_{x,y,q,w,P,D}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{n(\lambda)}}}\ket{x}\ket{y}\ket{q}\ket{w}\ket{P}\ket{D \cup(x, z) \cup(\bot, 0^n)}\ket{z}

Denote the two unnormalized states in the summation as
\ket{\eta_1},\ket{\eta_2}
respectively. Since
\sum_{x,y,q,w,P,D}\allowbreak|\alpha_{x,y,q,w,P,D}|^{2}=1
, we get
\norm{\ket{\eta_1}}^{2}\leq\frac{4}{2^{n}}
and thus
\norm{\pi\circ U_3\ket{\eta_1}}^{2}\leq\frac{4}{2^{n}}
. Now, we show
{\pi\circ U_{3}\ket{\eta_2}}=0
. Observe that once we apply
U_{3}
to
\ket{\eta_2}
, we get

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y,w,P,D:\\
|\mathsf{Inp}(D)\cap\overline{P}|\leq i\\
x\not\in D\\
P(x)=0\end{subarray}}\alpha_{x,y,q,w,P,D}\ket{x}\ket{y}\ket{q}\ket{w}\ket{P}\ket{D\cup\{(\bot, 0^n), (\bot, 0^n)\}}\ket{0^{n(\lambda)}}

Hence,
{\pi\circ U_{3}\ket{\eta_2}}=0
, which completes the proof.
∎

Now we prove
Theorem

15
.

Proof.

We will prove the operational version of the lemma (similar to
Definition

22
) and the actual incompressibility theorem directly follows (as in
Theorem

10
).

We will consider the compressed oracle simulation of the operational game, as in
Lemma

3
. Note that this simulation is purified: The output of
\mathcal{A}_{1}
is measured at the very end. Let
\mathsf{P}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{P}_{u(\lambda)}
denote the registers on which the predicates will appear - without loss of generality, we can assume that these registers exist from the beginning, and are initialized to zeroes (which will be interpreted as a predicate that rejects all inputs).

For any
j\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\pi_{j}
denote the projection onto database states (over the database register
\mathsf{D}
) and predicate states (over the registers
\mathsf{P}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{P}_{u(\lambda)}
) such that
|\mathsf{Inp}(D)\cap\overline{(\cup_{j=1}^{u(\lambda)}P_{j})}|>j
. Let
\ket{\tau}
be the state right before some query to the verification oracle, or the random oracle, or the section oracle, or right before updating a predicate register; and let
\ket{\tau'}
denote the state right after the execution of the query. We observe the following.

•

If the query is a verification query, then
\norm{\pi_j \ket{\tau'}}^{2}\leq\norm{\pi_j \ket{\tau}}^{2}+\frac{6}{\sqrt{2^{g_{2}(\lambda)}}}
. This follows directly from
Lemma

3
.

•

If the query is a section query, then
\norm{\pi_j \ket{\tau'}}^{2}\leq\norm{\pi_j \ket{\tau}}^{2}
. This follows since when a compressed oracle query is executed on the state
\ket{x}\ket{D}
, only
x
can be added to the database, no other values.

•

If the query is a normal query, then
\norm{\pi_{j+1} \ket{\tau'}}^{2}\leq\norm{\pi_j \ket{\tau}}^{2}
. This is because a single query can add at most one input to the database, and cannot change the other entries (other than deleting an entry).

•

If we are considering a point where a predicate register is being updated, then
\norm{\pi_j \ket{\tau'}}^{2}\leq\norm{\pi_j \ket{\tau}}^{2}
. This is because we are considering the union of the predicates in the definition of
\pi_{j}
, and adding more predicates makes the
good
set (
\mathsf{Inp}(D)\cap\overline{(\cup P_{j})}
) smaller.

Applying this to each query of the adversaries
\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2}
, it is easy to see that
\pi_{u(\lambda)}\ket{\eta}\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
where
\ket{\eta}
is the state of the system right before
\mathcal{A}_{2}
produces its output in the operational incompressibility game, since there is a total of
u(\lambda)
normal queries. By
Theorem

7
, this means that except with negligibly small probability,
\mathcal{A}_{2}
can output at most
u(\lambda)
different valid input - output pairs such that the input satisfies
P^{*}(x)=0
, which completes the proof of operational incompressibility.
∎

Part III
Quantum-Key Fire

8
Quantum Key-Fire for Signing in the Classical Oracle Model

In this section, we define quantum key-fire for signing in the classical oracle model.

For simplicity, we work with an oracle model where the oracle is sampled by
\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}.\mathsf{Setup}
(i.e. there is an independent oracle for each instance) and output as the serial number. However, we note that, through elementary tricks, constructions in this model can also be easily converted to the standard classical oracle model where there is a single global classical oracle
\mathcal{O}
(that is sampled from some distribution) that everyone (adversaries and the construction) has quantum-query access to, and
\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}.\mathsf{Setup}^{\mathcal{O}}
simply outputs a classical string as the verification key/serial number instead of outputting oracles. Again for simplicity, we consider the leakage phase as two adversaries communicating over a classical channel: note that this is equivalent to the leakage phase from the original game.

Definition 23

(Quantum Key-Fire for Signing in the Classical Oracle Model)
.

A public-key quantum key-fire scheme for signing
\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}
in the classical oracle model consists of the following efficient algorithms.

•

\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
: Takes in a security parameter, outputs an oracle
\mathcal{O}
as the verification key/serial number and a
key state

\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{flame}}
.

•

\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}.\mathsf{Clone}^{\mathcal{O}}(\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}})
: Takes in a key register
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}
, outputs a new register
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{clone}}
along with the input register
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}
.

•

\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}.\mathsf{Sign}^{\mathcal{O}}(\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}},m):
Takes in a key register and a message
m
, outputs a signature.

•

\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}.\mathsf{Ver}^{\mathcal{O}}(m,sig)
: Takes in a message and an alleged signature, outputs
1
or
0
.

Evaluation Correctness:

For all
m\in\mathcal{M}
,

\Pr[\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}.\mathsf{Ver}^{\mathcal{O}^{*}}(m,sig)=1:\begin{array}[]{c}{\mathcal{O}^{*}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}\leftarrow\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
sig\leftarrow\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}.\mathsf{Sign}^{\mathcal{O}^{*}}(\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}},m)\end{array}]\geq 1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

Strong cloning correctness:

This is defined the same as before: We require that output of
\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
is a pure state
\ket{\psi_{\ora^*}}
and that clone on input
\ket{\psi_{\ora^*}}
outputs two copies,
\ket{\psi_{\ora^*}}\otimes\ket{\psi_{\ora^*}}
.

Definition 24

(LOCC Leakage-Resilience Security for Quantum Key-Fire for Signing in the Classical Oracle Model)
.

Let
\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}
be a quantum key-fire scheme with classical oracles.
Consider the following game between an adversary
\mathcal{A}=(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2})
and a challenger.

\mathsf{LR-SIGN-GAME}_{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda})

1.

The challenger samples
sn,\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}\leftarrow\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
and submits
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}
to the adversary
\mathcal{A}_{1}
.

2.

Parse
\mathcal{O}=sn
.

3.

Leakage Phase:
The adversaries
\mathcal{A}_{1}^{\mathcal{O}}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\mathcal{O}}
interact classically by exchanging classical messages for any number of rounds.

4.

The challenger samples a random message
m^{*}\leftarrow\mathcal{M}
.

5.

The adversary
\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\mathcal{O}}
receives
m^{*}
, outputs
sig^{*}
.

6.

The challenger checks if
\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}.\mathsf{Ver}^{\mathcal{O}}(m^{*},sig^{*})=1
. If so, it outputs
1
, otherwise it outputs
0
.

We say that the quantum key-fire scheme
\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}
satisfies
LOCC leakage-resilience security
if for any QPT (or query bounded, in case of unconditional oracle-based schemes) adversary
\mathcal{A}
, we have

\Pr[\mathsf{LR-SIGN-GAME}_{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda})=1]\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

Note that this model also gives us the following conclusion. If the oracles of the scheme can be efficiently implemented (which is the case for our construction in
Section

9
, assuming one-way functions), our oracle security proof will also imply that our scheme is secure assuming virtual black-box obfuscation in the plain model and thus heuristically secure assuming indistinguishability obfuscation. Separately, as discussed, through elementary tricks our model also can be converted to the standard classical oracle model with unconditional security.

9
Construction

In this section, we give a quantum key-fire construction relative to a quantumly-accessible classical oracle (
Definition

23
) for signing
\nu(\lambda)
-bit messages (where
\nu(\lambda)
is superlogarithmic), then prove its signing and cloning correctness. The security is proven in
Section

10
.

In
Section

9.1
, we describe our building block: incompressible one-shot signatures (OSS). Then, in
Section

9.2
, we give our construction.

Before moving onto the building block and construction, we introduce some notation.

Oracle Algorithms

For a classical oracle
\mathcal{O}
, let
U_{\mathcal{O}}
denote its
query execution unitary
, that is, the unitary that maps
\ket{x}\ket{w}\ket{z}
to
\ket{x}\ket{w}\ket{z \oplus\ora(x)}
where the registers respectively are the query input, workspace and query output registers. For a quantum query algorithm
\mathcal{B}
, there is an associated
algorithm unitary

U_{\mathcal{B}}
, and the execution of
\mathcal{B}
with input
\mathsf{R}
and oracle access to
\mathcal{O}
is simply applying
(\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{O}}\cdot U_{\mathcal{B}})^{p(\lambda)}
to
(\mathsf{R},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux}})
(where
p(\lambda)
is some polynomial and
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux}}
is initialized to zeroes), and outputting (the whole or part of) the resulting register (after possibly measuring some parts of it in the computational basis)

9.1
Building Block: Incompressible One-Shot Signature Scheme

We will use an incompressible one-shot signature scheme
\mathsf{OSS}
for
1
-bit messages relative to a classical oracle (
Definition

21
) as a building block.

We make the following assumptions about
\mathsf{OSS}
for presentation clarity, and all of these are without loss of generality.

Let
t_{1}(\cdot),t_{2}(\cdot),t_{3}(\cdot),t_{4}(\cdot)
be some polynomials.

•

Since
\mathsf{OSS}
is an incompressible OSS, it has split oracles (
Definition

20
): write
(\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}}
,
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}}
,
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}})=\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}}
to denote its oracles. Let
U_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}}
be the algorithm unitary and let
U_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}}}
be the query execution unitary for
\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}
, and similarly define
U_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}}
,
U_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}}}
for
\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}
.

•

\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}^{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}}}(1^{\lambda})
: Define
U^{{}^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}},\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}}=(U_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}}}\cdot U_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}})^{t_{2}(\lambda)}
. Then, this algorithm proceeds as follows:

1.

Introduce the auxiliary input register
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{inp-aux}}
initialized to
\ket{0^{\auxsizeosskey(\lambda)}}
.

2.

Apply
U^{{}^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}},\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}}
to
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{inp-aux}}
and parse the output into the registers:
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{out-aux}}
.

3.

Measure
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}}
to obtain the verification key
vk
.

4.

Output
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}
as the signing key and
vk
as the verification key.

•

\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}^{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}}}(vk,\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{sk}},m)
: Define
U^{{}^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}}=(U_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}}}\cdot U_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}})^{t_{1}(\lambda)}
. Then, this algorithm proceeds as follows:

1.

Introduce the auxiliary input register
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{inp-aux}}
initialized to
\ket{0^{\auxsizeossign(\lambda)}}
.

2.

Introduce the message register
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}}
initialized to
\ket{m}
.

3.

Introduce the verification key register
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}}
initialized to
\ket{vk}
.

4.

Apply
U^{{}^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}}
to
(\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{sk}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{inp-aux}})
and parse the output into the registers:
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}}
,
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}}
,
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{sig}}
,
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{out-aux}}
(registers
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}}
,
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}}
are unmodified).

5.

Measure
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{sig}}
to obtain the signature
sig
.

6.

Output
sig
.

•

\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}^{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}}}(vk,m,sig)
: Applies the oracle
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}}
to obtain the output
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}}(vk,m,sig)
. If the output is
1
, the algorithm outputs
1
, otherwise it outputs
0
25
25

25

The assumption about
\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}
is essentially that the signature verification procedure is classical and deterministic. It is easy to see that deterministic verification assumption is without loss of generality due to correctness with overwhelming probability. When we do not require the oracles to be efficiently implementable, classical verification assumption is also without loss of generality since any
classical input - classical output
quantum algorithm can be simulated by a classical oracle. However, in the efficient oracle case, the assumption about
\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}
might not be without loss of generality, but it is nevertheless true for our OSS construction in
Section

6
. The assumptions about
\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}
and
\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}
are indeed without loss of generality in all cases.
.

9.2
Construction

Let
\nu(\lambda)
be any polynomially bounded function. We now give our public-key quantum key-fire construction for signing
\nu(\lambda)
-bit messages, with the classical oracle
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}}=(\mathcal{O}_{0}
,
\mathcal{O}_{1}
,
\mathcal{O}_{2}
,
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}}
,
\mathcal{O}_{3},\mathcal{O}_{4})
.

Let
\mathsf{OSS}
be a one-shot signature scheme for
1
-bit messages as described in
Section

9.1
. Let
p_{1}(\lambda)
denote the length of verification keys of
\mathsf{OSS}
,
p_{2}(\lambda)
denote the input length of the oracle
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}}
.

\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})

1.

Sample random functions
H_{0},H_{1},H_{\mathsf{sig}}
where
H_{0}:\{0,1\}^{p_{1}(\lambda)+p_{2}(\lambda)}\to\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
,
H_{1}:\{0,1\}^{p_{1}(\lambda)+p_{2}(\lambda)}\to\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
.

2.

Sample random function
H_{\mathsf{sig}}:\{0,1\}^{\nu(\lambda)}\to\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
.

3.

Sample
(\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}}
,
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}}
,
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}})\leftarrow\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenOracles}(1^{\lambda})
.

4.

Construct the oracle
\mathcal{O}_{0}
.

\mathcal{O}_{0}(ivk,isig,z)

Hardcoded:
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}},H_{0}

1.

Check if
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}}(ivk,0,isig)=1
. If not, output
\bot
and terminate.

2.

Output
H_{0}({ivk}||z)
.

5.

Construct the oracle
\mathcal{O}_{1}
.

\mathcal{O}_{1}(ivk,isig,z)

Hardcoded:
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}},H_{1}

1.

Check if
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}}(ivk,1,isig)=1
. If not, output
\bot
and terminate.

2.

Output
H_{1}({ivk}||z)
.

6.

Construct the oracle
\mathcal{O}_{2}
.

\mathcal{O}_{2}(ivk,y_{0},y_{1},z)

Hardcoded:
H_{0},H_{1},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}}

1.

Check if
y_{0}=H_{0}(ivk||z)
and if
y_{1}=H_{1}(ivk||z)
. If not, output
\bot
and terminate.

2.

Output
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}}(z)
.

7.

Construct the oracle
\mathcal{O}_{3}
.

\mathcal{O}_{3}(ivk,y_{0},y_{1},m)

Hardcoded:
H_{0},H_{1},H_{\mathsf{sig}}

1.

Check if
y_{0}=H_{0}(ivk||m)
and if
y_{1}=H_{1}(ivk||m)
. If not, output
\bot
and terminate.

2.

Output
H_{\mathsf{sig}}(m)
.

8.

Construct the oracle
\mathcal{O}_{4}
.

\mathcal{O}_{4}(m,y)

Hardcoded:
H_{\mathsf{sig}}

1.

Output
1
if
H_{\mathsf{sig}}(m)=y
, and output
0
otherwise.

9.

Set
sn=(\mathcal{O}_{0},\mathcal{O}_{1},\mathcal{O}_{2},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{3},\mathcal{O}_{4})
.

10.

Simulate the purified version of
\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}^{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}}}(1^{\lambda})
and let
(\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{out-aux}})
be the output registers.

11.

Output
sn,(\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{out-aux}})
.

\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}.\mathsf{Sign}^{\mathcal{O}}(\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{flame}},m)

1.

Parse
(\mathcal{O}_{0},\mathcal{O}_{1},\mathcal{O}_{2},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}},\mathcal{O}_{3},\mathcal{O}_{4})=\mathcal{O}
.

2.

Parse
(\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{out-aux}})=\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{flame}}
.

3.

Measure
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}}
to obtain
ivk
.

4.

Coherently run the following to obtain
y_{0}
and then rewind (as in
Lemma

1
):

1.

Simulate
\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}^{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}}}(ivk,\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}},0)
, let
isig
be the output. Run
\mathcal{O}_{0}(ivk,isig,m)
and let
y_{0}
be the output.

5.

Coherently run the following to obtain
y_{1}
and then rewind (as in
Lemma

1
):

1.

Simulate
\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}^{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}}}(ivk,\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}},0)
, let
isig
be the output. Run
\mathcal{O}_{1}(ivk,isig,m)
and let
y_{1}
be the output.

6.

Output
\mathcal{O}_{3}(ivk,y_{0},y_{1},m)
.

\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}.\mathsf{Ver}^{\mathcal{O}}(m,sig)

1.

Parse
(\mathcal{O}_{0},\mathcal{O}_{1},\mathcal{O}_{2},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{3},\mathcal{O}_{4})=\mathcal{O}
.

2.

Output
\mathcal{O}_{4}(m,sig)
.

\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}.\mathsf{Clone}^{\mathcal{O}}(\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{flame}})

1.

Parse
(\mathcal{O}_{0},\mathcal{O}_{1},\mathcal{O}_{2},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{3},\mathcal{O}_{4})=\mathcal{O}
.

2.

Parse
(\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{out-aux,1}})=\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{flame}}
.

3.

Create the registers
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,2}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,3}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,4}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,5}}
. Initialize
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,2}}
and
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,3}}
to
\ket{0^{\auxsizeossign(\lambda)}}
and
\ket{0^{\auxsizeosskey(\lambda)}}
. Initialize
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,4}}
and
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,5}}
to
\ket{0^\lambda}
. Initialize
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}}
to
\ket{0}
. At some steps, we will parse
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,3}}
as
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-inp,3}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,3}},\mathsf{R}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{aux,3}}

\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}
, where

reads the oracle input input from
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-inp,3}}
and writes the oracle output onto
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,3}}
.

4.

For
t_{2}(\lambda)
steps, execute the following.

1.

Apply
U_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}}
to
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,3}}
.

2.

Execute the following (which essentially simulates the oracle call to
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}}
):

1.

Apply

to
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}}
,
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}}
,
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}
,
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,2}}
. Parse the output as
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}}
,
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}}
,
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{sig}}
,
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{out-aux,2}}
as in
\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}
.

2.

Apply
U_{\mathcal{O}_{0}}
with input registers
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{sig}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-inp,3}}
and the output register
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,4}}
.

3.

Apply
(U^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}})^{-1}
to
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{sig}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,2}}
, parse the output as
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}}
,
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}}
,
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}
,
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,2}}
.

4.

Set
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}}
to
\ket{1}
.

5.

Apply
U^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}}
to
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,2}}
. Parse the output as
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}}
,
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}}
,
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{sig}}
,
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{out-aux,2}}
as in
\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}
.

6.

Apply
U_{\mathcal{O}_{1}}
with input registers
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{sig}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-inp,3}}
and the output register

.

with input registers

Apply

with input registers
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{sig}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-inp,3}}
and the output register
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,5}}
.

Apply
(U^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}})^{-1}

,\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{sig}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,2}}

,

,
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,2}}
.

Set
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}}

,

,
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,2}}

Apply

with input registers

and the output register
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,4}}
.

Apply
(U^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}})^{-1}
to
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{sig}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,2}}

,

\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,2}}
.

Parse

as

as in

.

Set

.

Remark 1
.

As described above, the signing algorithm
\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}.\mathsf{Sign}
of our scheme consumes the flame state given to it. Note that thanks to the strong clonability correctness of the scheme, this is not a issue since one can clone the flame state before signing a message. However, if one insists on not consuming/altering the flame state while signing, this is also possible: Using the gentle measurement technique (
Lemma

1
) it is also easy to see that the signing algorithm can be made so that it does not alter the state of the flame register (except up to negligible trace distance), since the output is pseudodeterministic (it is equal to
H_{\mathsf{sig}}(m)
with overwhelming probability).

Remark 2
.

Aside from the conversion from quantum protection schemes for signing to quantum protection schemes for general unlearnable functionalities shown by the previous work, we can also slightly modify our construction above to achieve key-fire for unlearnable functionalities directly: Instead of
\mathcal{O}_{3},\mathcal{O}_{4}
, we will just have a new
\mathcal{O}_{3}
which outputs
f^{*}(x)
after verifying
y_{0},y_{1}
. The proof also follows the same, with the difference that at the final hybrid, instead of invoking
Theorem

21
, we simply invoke the unlearnability of the functionality.

We claim that the construction satisfies correctness and security.

Theorem 16
.

\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}
satisfies quantum key-fire signing correctness, strong cloning correctness (
Definition

18
). Let
\nu(\lambda)
be superlogarithmic. Suppose
\mathsf{OSS}
satisfies
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}}
incompressibility and one-shot security, then
\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}
satisfies unbounded (i.e. non-interactive) leakage-resilience for signing unconditionally. Suppose
\mathsf{OSS}
satisfies strong incompressibility and one-shot security, then
\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}
satisfies LOCC leakage-resilience for signing (
Definition

24
) unconditionally. Further, the oracles of
\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}
can be implemented efficiently using a one-way function while preserving security (in which case, the security becomes computational rather than information-theoretic).

We prove the signing correctness and cloning correctness in
Sections

9.3
and
9.4
, and we prove security in
Section

10
.

By implementing our construction using the incompressible one-shot signature we construct in
Theorem

9
, we get the following corollary.

Theorem 17
.

Relative to a classical oracle, there exists a quantum key-fire scheme that satisfies LOCC leakage-resilience for signing functionality.

Theorem 18
.

Relative to a classical oracle, there exists a quantum key-fire scheme that satisfies LOCC leakage-resilience for any unlearnable functionality.

9.3
Proof of Signing Correctness

In this section, we prove the signing correctness of our scheme.

For simplicity, we will assume that
\mathsf{OSS}
satisfies perfect correctness, and we will show that
\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}
satisfies perfect signing correctness. Through elementary lemmas, this implies that if
\mathsf{OSS}
satisfies correctness with overwhelming probability, then
\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}
also satisfies signing correctness with overwhelming probability.

We first observe that the flame states of our scheme (
sn,\ket{\psi}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{out-aux}}}
) output by
\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
are the same as the purified output of
\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}
(whose original version traces out
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{out-aux}}
and measures
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}}
, but
\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}.\mathsf{Setup}
does neither since it is purified). Then,
\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}.\mathsf{Sign}
first measures
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}}
to obtain some value
ivk
and (effectively) traces out
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{out-aux}}
. This collapses the state to the one-shot signature key with the serial number
ivk
. By signing correctness of
\mathsf{OSS}
and definition of
\mathcal{O}_{0}
, we get
y_{0}=H_{0}(ivk||m)
with probability
1
. Thus, by
Lemma

1
, the signing key is indeed not altered since we are rewinding. Then again by signing correctness of
\mathsf{OSS}
and definition of
\mathcal{O}_{1}
, we get
y_{1}=H_{1}(ivk||m)
with probability
1
. Hence, the output of the signing algorithm is
\mathcal{O}_{3}(ivk,H_{0}(ivk||m),H_{1}(ivk||m),m)
which is equal to
H_{\mathsf{sig}}(m)
. Due to the definition of the verification algorithm, this completes the proof of signing correctness.

9.4
Proof of Strong Cloning Correctness

In this section, we prove the strong cloning correctness of our scheme.

First, we note that the flame states of our scheme are indeed pure states as required for strong cloning correctness. Observe that the state output by
\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}.\mathsf{Setup}
is simply
\ket{\psi_{flame}}=(U_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}}}\cdot U_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}})^{t_{2}(\lambda)}\ket{0^{\auxsizeosskey(\lambda)}}
. This is also the same as the purified output of
\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}
(whose not-purified version traces out
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{out-aux}}
and measures
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}}
, but
\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}.\mathsf{Setup}
does neither).

For simplicity, we will assume that
\mathsf{OSS}
satisfies perfect correctness, and we will show that
\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}
satisfies perfect strong cloning correctness. Through elementary lemmas, this implies that if
\mathsf{OSS}
satisfies correctness with overwhelming probability, then
\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}
also satisfies strong cloning correctness with overwhelming probability.

We first prove the following lemma.

Lemma 4
.

During the loop in
\mathsf{QKeyFireSign}.\mathsf{Clone}
, if the state over the registers
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}}
,
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}
,
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{out-aux,1}}
,
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}}
,
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,2}}
,
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,3}}
,
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,4}}
,
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,5}}
is

\ket{\psi_{flame}}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{out-aux,1}}}\otimes\ket{0}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}}}\otimes\ket{0^{p_2(\lambda)}}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,2}}}\otimes\ket{\zeta}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,3}}}\otimes\ket{0^{\lambda}}_{{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,4}}}}\otimes\ket{0^{\lambda}}_{{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,5}}}}

at the beginning of an iteration, then the state at the end of that iteration is

\ket{\psi_{flame}}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{out-aux,1}}}\otimes\ket{0}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}}}\otimes\ket{0^{p_2(\lambda)}}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,2}}}\otimes(U_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}}}\cdot U_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}}\ket{\zeta})\otimes\ket{0^{\lambda}}_{{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,4}}}}\otimes\ket{0^{\lambda}}_{{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,5}}}}.

Proof.

We will show the result by writing the state of the registers after each step of the iteration. The actual order of the registers will stay the same throughout, however, when writing the state we will reorder some registers for convenience of notation.

We also introduce the following notation for conciseness.

•

\ket{\psi_{flame}}=\sum_{vk,w}\beta_{vk,w}\ket{vk}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}}}\otimes\ket{\phi_{vk,w}}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}}\otimes\ket{w}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{out-aux}}}
,

•

U_{1}=U_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}}
,

•

U_{2}=U_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}}}
,

•

U=U_{2}\cdot U_{1}
,

•

U_{1}\ket{\zeta}=\sum_{z,o,w^{\prime}}\gamma_{z,o,w^{\prime}}\ket{z}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-inp,3}}}\otimes\ket{o}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,3}}}\otimes\ket{w'}_{\mathsf{R}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{aux,3}}}
.

Now we show the evolution of the state during the loop.

1.

After applying
U_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{GenQKey}}
to
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,3}}
, we get

\ket{\psi_{flame}}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{out-aux,1}}}\otimes\ket{0}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}}}\otimes\ket{0^{p_2(\lambda)}}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,2}}}\otimes(U_{1}\ket{\zeta})_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,3}}}\otimes\ket{0^{\lambda}}_{{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,4}}}}\otimes\ket{0^{\lambda}}_{{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,5}}}}

2.

After applying
U^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}}
to
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,2}}
, we get

\ket{0}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}}}\otimes\left(\sum_{vk,w}\beta_{vk,w}\ket{vk}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}}}\otimes\sum_{sig}\alpha_{vk,w,sig}\ket{sig}\right)\otimes(U_{1}\ket{\zeta})_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,3}}}\otimes\ket{0^{\lambda}}_{{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,4}}}}\otimes\ket{0^{\lambda}}_{{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,5}}}}

where for each
vk,w,sig
with
\beta_{vk,w}\neq 0
and
\alpha_{vk,w,sig}\neq 0
we have that
\mathcal{O}^{*}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}}(vk,sig,0)=1
by correctness of
\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}
.

3.

After applying
U_{\mathcal{O}_{0}}
with input registers
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{sig}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-inp,3}}
and the output register
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,4}}
, we get

\displaystyle\ket{0}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}}}\otimes\bigg(

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}vk,w\\
z,o,w^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\beta_{vk,w}\ket{vk}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}}}\otimes(\sum_{sig}\alpha_{vk,w,sig})\gamma_{z,o,w^{\prime}}\ket{z}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-inp,3}}}\otimes

\displaystyle\ket{o}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,3}}}\otimes\ket{\ora_0(vk, sig_{vk,w}, z)}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,4}}}\otimes\ket{w'}_{\mathsf{R}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{aux,3}}}\bigg)\otimes\ket{0^{\lambda}}_{{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,5}}}}

which is equal to

\displaystyle\ket{0}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}}}\otimes

\displaystyle\Bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}vk,w\end{subarray}}\beta_{vk,w}\left(\ket{vk}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}}}\otimes\big(\sum_{z,o,w^{\prime}}\gamma_{z,o,w^{\prime}}\ket{z}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-inp,3}}}\ket{o}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,3}}}\ket{H_0(vk, z)}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,4}}}\otimes\ket{w'}_{\mathsf{R}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{aux,3}}}\big)\right)\otimes

\displaystyle\ket{sig_{vk,w}}\Bigg)\otimes\ket{0^{\lambda}}_{{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,5}}}}

4.

After applying
(U^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}})^{-1}
to
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{crs}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{sig}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,2}}
, we get

\displaystyle\ket{0}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}}}\otimes

\displaystyle\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}vk,w\end{subarray}}\beta_{vk,w}\left(\ket{vk}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}}}\otimes\big(\sum_{z,o,w^{\prime}}\gamma_{z,o,w^{\prime}}\ket{z}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-inp,3}}}\ket{o}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,3}}}\ket{H_0(vk, z)}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,4}}}\otimes\ket{w'}_{\mathsf{R}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{aux,3}}}\big)\right)\otimes

\displaystyle\ket{\phi_{vk,w}}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}}\otimes\ket{w}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{out-aux}}}\otimes\ket{0^{p_2(\lambda)}}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,2}}}\bigg)\otimes\ket{0^{\lambda}}_{{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,5}}}}

5.

After applying
X
to
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}}
, this register changes from
\ket{0}
to
\ket{1}
, and the others are unaffected.

6.

After applying
U^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}}
to
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,2}}
we get

\displaystyle\ket{1}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}}}\otimes

\displaystyle\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}vk,w\end{subarray}}\beta_{vk,w}\left(\ket{vk}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}}}\otimes\big(\sum_{z,o,w^{\prime}}\gamma_{z,o,w^{\prime}}\ket{z}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-inp,3}}}\ket{o}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,3}}}\ket{H_0(vk, z)}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,4}}}\otimes\ket{w'}_{\mathsf{R}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{aux,3}}}\big)\right)\otimes

\displaystyle\sum_{sig}\alpha^{\prime}_{vk,w,sig}\ket{sig}\bigg)\otimes\ket{0^{\lambda}}_{{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,5}}}}

where for each
vk,w,sig
with
\beta_{vk,w}\neq 0
and
\alpha^{\prime}_{vk,w,sig}\neq 0
we have that
\mathcal{O}^{*}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}}(vk,sig,1)=1
by correctness of
\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}
.

7.

After applying
U_{\mathcal{O}_{1}}
with input registers
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{sig}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-inp,3}}
and the output register
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,5}}
, we get

\displaystyle\ket{1}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}}}\otimes

\displaystyle\Bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}vk,w\end{subarray}}\beta_{vk,w}\bigg(\ket{vk}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}}}\otimes\big(\sum_{z,o,w^{\prime}}\gamma_{z,o,w^{\prime}}\ket{z}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-inp,3}}}\ket{o}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,3}}}\ket{H_0(vk, z)}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,4}}}\ket{H_1(vk,z)}_{{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,5}}}}\otimes

\displaystyle\ket{w'}_{\mathsf{R}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{aux,3}}}\big)\bigg)\otimes\sum_{sig}\alpha^{\prime}_{vk,w,sig}\ket{sig}\Bigg)

8.

After applying
U_{\mathcal{O}_{2}}
with input registers
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,4}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,5}}
and the output register
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,3}}
, we get

\displaystyle\ket{1}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}}}\otimes

\displaystyle\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}vk,w\end{subarray}}\beta_{vk,w}\Bigg(\ket{vk}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}}}\otimes\big(\sum_{z,o,w^{\prime}}\gamma_{z,o,w^{\prime}}\ket{z}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-inp,3}}}\ket{o \oplus\ora^*_{\oss.\genqkey(z)}}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,3}}}\ket{H_0(vk, z)}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,4}}}

\displaystyle\ket{H_1(vk,z)}_{{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,5}}}}\otimes\ket{w'}_{\mathsf{R}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{aux,3}}}\big)\Bigg)\otimes\sum_{sig}\alpha^{\prime}_{vk,w,sig}\ket{sig}\bigg)

9.

After applying
U_{\mathcal{O}_{1}}
with input registers
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{sig}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-inp,3}}
and the output register
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,5}}
, we get

\displaystyle\ket{1}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}}}\otimes

\displaystyle\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}vk,w\end{subarray}}\beta_{vk,w}\Bigg(\ket{vk}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}}}\otimes\big(\sum_{z,o,w^{\prime}}\gamma_{z,o,w^{\prime}}\ket{z}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-inp,3}}}\ket{o \oplus\ora^*_{\oss.\genqkey(z)}}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,3}}}\ket{H_0(vk, z)}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,4}}}

\displaystyle\ket{0^{\lambda}}_{{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,5}}}}\otimes\ket{w'}_{\mathsf{R}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{aux,3}}}\big)\Bigg)\otimes\sum_{sig}\alpha^{\prime}_{vk,w,sig}\ket{sig}\bigg)

10.

After applying
(U^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}})^{-1}
to
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{sig}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,2}}
, we get

\displaystyle\ket{1}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}}}\otimes

\displaystyle\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}vk,w\end{subarray}}\beta_{vk,w}\Bigg(\ket{vk}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}}}\otimes\big(\sum_{z,o,w^{\prime}}\gamma_{z,o,w^{\prime}}\ket{z}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-inp,3}}}\ket{o \oplus\ora^*_{\oss.\genqkey(z)}}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,3}}}\ket{H_0(vk, z)}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,4}}}

\displaystyle\ket{0^{\lambda}}_{{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,5}}}}\otimes\ket{w'}_{\mathsf{R}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{aux,3}}}\big)\Bigg)\otimes\ket{\phi_{vk,w}}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}}\otimes\ket{w}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{out-aux,1}}}\otimes\ket{0^{p_2(\lambda)}}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,2}}}\bigg)

11.

After applying
X
to
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}}
, this register changes from
\ket{1}
to
\ket{0}
, and the others are unaffected.

12.

After applying
U^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}}
to
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{crs}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,2}}
, we get

\displaystyle\ket{0}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}}}\otimes

\displaystyle\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}vk,w\end{subarray}}\beta_{vk,w}\Bigg(\ket{vk}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}}}\otimes\big(\sum_{z,o,w^{\prime}}\gamma_{z,o,w^{\prime}}\ket{z}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-inp,3}}}\ket{o \oplus\ora^*_{\oss.\genqkey(z)}}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,3}}}\ket{H_0(vk, z)}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,4}}}

\displaystyle\ket{0^{\lambda}}_{{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,5}}}}\otimes\ket{w'}_{\mathsf{R}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{aux,3}}}\big)\Bigg)\otimes\sum_{sig}\alpha_{vk,w,sig}\ket{sig}\bigg)

where for each
vk,w,sig
with
\beta_{vk,w}\neq 0
and
\alpha_{vk,w,sig}\neq 0
we have that
\mathcal{O}^{*}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}}(crs^{*},vk,sig,0)=1
by correctness of
\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}
.

13.

After applying
U_{\mathcal{O}_{0}}
with input registers
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{sig}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-inp,3}}
and the output register
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,4}}
, we get

\displaystyle\ket{0}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}}}\otimes

\displaystyle\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}vk,w\end{subarray}}\beta_{vk,w}\Bigg(\ket{vk}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}}}\otimes\big(\sum_{z,o,w^{\prime}}\gamma_{z,o,w^{\prime}}\ket{z}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-inp,3}}}\ket{o \oplus\ora^*_{\oss.\genqkey(z)}}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,3}}}\ket{0^\lambda}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,4}}}

\displaystyle\ket{0^{\lambda}}_{{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,5}}}}\otimes\ket{w'}_{\mathsf{R}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{aux,3}}}\big)\Bigg)\otimes\sum_{sig}\alpha_{vk,w,sig}\ket{sig}\bigg)

14.

After applying
(U^{\prime}_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}})^{-1}
to
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{sig}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,2}}
, we get

\displaystyle\ket{0}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}}}\otimes

\displaystyle\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}vk,w\end{subarray}}\beta_{vk,w}\Bigg(\ket{vk}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}}}\otimes\big(\sum_{z,o,w^{\prime}}\gamma_{z,o,w^{\prime}}\ket{z}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-inp,3}}}\ket{o \oplus\ora^*_{\oss.\genqkey(z)}}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,3}}}\ket{0^\lambda}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,4}}}

\displaystyle\ket{0^{\lambda}}_{{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,5}}}}\otimes\ket{w'}_{\mathsf{R}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{aux,3}}}\big)\Bigg)\otimes\ket{\phi_{vk,w}}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}}}\otimes\ket{w}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{out-aux,1}}}\otimes\ket{0^{p_2(\lambda)}}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,2}}}\bigg)

which is equal to

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{flame}}\otimes\ket{0}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}}}\otimes\ket{0^{p_2(\lambda)}}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,2}}}\otimes U_{2}(U_{1}\ket{\zeta})\otimes\ket{0^{\lambda}}_{{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,4}}}}\otimes\ket{0^{\lambda}}_{{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,5}}}}=

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{flame}}\otimes\ket{0}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}}}\otimes\ket{0^{p_2(\lambda)}}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,2}}}\otimes U\ket{\zeta}\otimes\ket{0^{\lambda}}_{{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,4}}}}\otimes\ket{0^{\lambda}}_{{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,5}}}}

as claimed.

∎

It is easy to see that the strong cloning correctness follows easily with this lemma: The loop starts with the state

\ket{\psi}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{out-aux,1}}}\otimes\ket{0}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}}}\otimes\ket{0^{p_2(\lambda)}}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,2}}}\otimes\ket{0^{p_2(\lambda)}}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,3}}}\otimes\ket{0^{\lambda}}_{{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,4}}}}\otimes\ket{0^{\lambda}}_{{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,5}}}},

thus, at the end, we get the state

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{flame}}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{out-aux,1}}}\otimes\ket{0}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}}}\otimes\ket{0^{p_2(\lambda)}}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,2}}}\otimes(U^{p_{1}(\lambda)}\ket{0^{p_2(\lambda)}})_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,3}}}\otimes

\displaystyle\ket{0^{\lambda}}_{{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,4}}}}\otimes\ket{0^{\lambda}}_{{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,5}}}}=

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{flame}}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{vk}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{key}},\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{out-aux,1}}}\otimes\ket{0}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{msg}}}\otimes\ket{0^{p_2(\lambda)}}_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,2}}}\otimes(\ket{\psi})_{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{aux,3}}}\otimes\ket{0^{\lambda}}_{{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,4}}}}\otimes\ket{0^{\lambda}}_{{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{ora-out,5}}}}.

Hence, the algorithm outputs
\ket{\psi_{flame}}\otimes\ket{\psi_{flame}}
.

10
Proof of Security of the Main Construction

In this section, we prove the security of our scheme. Throughout the proof, we will refer to the adversary that gets the flame state (
\mathcal{A}_{1}
) as the sender adversary and to the adversary that outputs the forged signature as the receiver adversary (
\mathcal{A}_{2}
).

Suppose for a contradiction that there exists a query-bounded adversary
\mathcal{A}=(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2})
such that
\Pr[\mathsf{LR-SIGN-GAME}_{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda})=1]>\frac{1}{q^{*}(\lambda)}
for some polynomial
q^{*}(\cdot)
and infinitely many values
\lambda>0
. Note that
\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2}
will have query access to the oracles
\mathcal{O}_{0},\mathcal{O}_{1},\mathcal{O}_{2},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}},\mathcal{O}_{3},\mathcal{O}_{4}
of the construction.

Adversary Assumptions

Without loss of generality, we make the following assumptions about the adversaries
\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2}
(where
u(\lambda),t(\lambda)
are some polynomials), and we also define some notation.

•

(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2})
runs for exactly
u(\lambda)
rounds with probability
1
, where each round consists of a message from
\mathcal{A}_{1}
to
\mathcal{A}_{2}
and then a message from
\mathcal{A}_{2}
to
\mathcal{A}_{1}
(except for the last round where
\mathcal{A}_{2}
outputs
\bot
as its message).

•

We will denote the
i
-round of
\mathcal{A}_{1}
as
\mathcal{A}_{1,i}
, for
i\in[u(\lambda)]
. That is, at round
i
,
\mathcal{A}_{1}
simply executes
\mathcal{A}_{1,i}
on its internal state register to obtain its message and the updated internal state register.
\mathcal{A}_{1,i}
makes a total of
t(\lambda)
queries to the oracles.

•

Similar to above, we will denote the
i
-th round of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
as
\mathcal{A}_{2,i}
for
i\in[u(\lambda)]
. Note that for
\mathcal{A}_{2,u(\lambda)}
, the message output by it is
\bot
since there is no message from
\mathcal{A}_{2}
to
\mathcal{A}_{1}
. Finally, we will write
\mathcal{A}_{2,\mathsf{final}}
to denote the challenge phase of the adversary
\mathcal{A}_{2}
: It receives the internal state register produced by
\mathcal{A}_{2,u(\lambda)}
and the challenge message to be signed from the challenger and outputs a signature.

•

Every round (i.e. for all
\ell\in[u(\lambda)]
), queries of
\mathcal{A}_{2,i}
to the oracles
\mathcal{O}_{0},\mathcal{O}_{1},\mathcal{O}_{2}
proceeds in triples: It first makes one query to
\mathcal{O}_{0}
, then to
\mathcal{O}_{1}
, and then to
\mathcal{O}_{2}
. We will call these groups of three queries a
query-triplet
. Further, we assume that each round,
\mathcal{A}_{2,i}
has exactly
t(\lambda)
query-triplets, with any (polynomial) number of queries to the other oracles in between the triplets but with a total of exactly
t(\lambda)
queries to each of the oracles
\mathcal{O}_{3},\mathcal{O}_{4},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Ver}}
during the round. Note that this means that
\mathcal{A}_{2}
has a total of
u(\lambda)\cdot t(\lambda)
query-triplets and a total of
2u(\lambda)\cdot t(\lambda)
over the leakage protocol.

Note that without loss of generality, we can take
t(\lambda)=1
by modifying our adversaries, since we do not impose an a-priori bound on the number of rounds. Similarly, without loss of generality, we can assume that each round, queries of
\mathcal{A}_{2,i}
to
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{3},\mathcal{O}_{4}
occur in this order, after its queries to
\mathcal{O}_{0},\mathcal{O}_{1},\mathcal{O}_{2}
.

Organization of the proof

Our proof will be done through a sequence of hybrids,
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
(which is the original game
\mathsf{LR-SIGN-GAME}_{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda})
) through
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
(with some additional subhybrids in between). In the remainder of this section, we simply state our main theorems: We state our theorem about the indistinguishability of the first and the last hybrid (
Theorem

19
), and about the security in the final hybrid
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
(
Theorem

20
). Then, combining these main theorems simply proves the security of our scheme. The main theorems then are then proven in the subsequent subsections. In more detail, our proof is organized as follows.

In
Section

10.1
, we define various modified adversaries that we use in our hybrids. Then, in
Section

10.2
, we give the definitions of our hybrids. Then in
Section

10.3
, we prove
Theorem

19
, that is, we show the indistinguishability of our hybrids (except for
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2,\ell,0}
,
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2,\ell,1}
which we prove in
Section

11
). Finally, in
Section

10.4
, we prove
Theorem

20
, that is, we show the security in the final hybrid.

We now give our main theorem statements about the hybrids (which are defined in
Section

10.2
).

Theorem 19
.

\left|\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}-\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}\right|\leq\frac{1}{2\cdot q^{*}(\lambda)}

We prove this theorem in
Section

10.3
.

Theorem 20
.

\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}=1]\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
.

We prove this theorem in
Section

10.4
.

Now we complete the proof of security assuming these two theorems. We had assumed for a contradiction that
\Pr[\mathsf{LR-SIGN-GAME}_{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda})=1]=\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}=1]>\frac{1}{q^{*}(\lambda)}
for some polynomial
q^{*}(\cdot)
and infinitely many values
\lambda>0
. Combining this with
Theorem

19
, we get that
\Pr[\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}=1]>\frac{1}{2q^{*}(\lambda)}
for infinitely many
\lambda>0
. However, this is a contradiction by
Theorem

20
. Therefore, our contradiction hypothesis is wrong, and thus we get
\Pr[\mathsf{LR-SIGN-GAME}_{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda})=1]\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
, completing the proof of security.

10.1
Defining Modified Adversaries

In this section, we define the modified adversaries we will be using in the hybrids. We will explicitly denote the section queries to the oracles
H_{0},H_{1}
.

We define
\mathcal{A}_{1,u(\lambda)+1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2,u(\lambda)+1}
as follows.

\mathcal{C}_{2,\ell}^{\mathcal{O}_{sign}^{\prime},\mathcal{O}_{3},\mathcal{O}_{4}}(Y)
to obtain
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{internal}},S
.

4.

Output
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{internal}},S
.

\mathcal{Q}
-Adversaries

For
\ell\in[u(\lambda)+1]
, we define the adversaries
\mathcal{Q}_{1,\ell}
,
\mathcal{Q}_{2,\ell}
.

\mathcal{Q}_{1,\ell}^{(\mathcal{O}_{0},\mathcal{O}_{1},\mathcal{O}_{2},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{3},\mathcal{O}_{4})}(T,L)

1.

Set
X_{3}
to be the empty list.

2.

Set
D_{3}
to be empty database.

3.

G\leftarrow\mathcal{R}_{1,\ell}^{(\mathcal{O}_{0},\mathcal{O}_{1},\mathcal{O}_{2},\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{OSS}.\mathsf{Sign}},\mathcal{O}_{3},\mathcal{O}_{4})}(T,L)
.

4.

For
i\in[512\cdot\lambda\cdot(t^{2}(\lambda)\cdot\ell)\cdot\lambda^{4}\cdot(u(\lambda)\cdot q^{*}(\lambda)\cdot t(\lambda))^{2}]
:

1.

Sample a random index
ind
between
1
and the number of oracle queries by
\mathcal{R}_{2,\ell}
to
\mathcal{O}_{3}
.

2.

Simulate
\mathcal{R}_{2,\ell}^{\mathcal{O}_{3},\mathcal{O}_{4}}(G{})
until right before its
ind
-th oracle query to
\mathcal{O}_{3}
is executed and measure the query input register instead of executing the query. Let
ivk,y_{0},y_{1},m
be the measurement outcome. If
\mathcal{O}_{3}(ivk,y_{0},y_{1},m)\neq\bot
, add
(ivk,y_{0},y_{1},m)
to

and set

.

5.

Output
(G{},(X_{3},D_{3}))
.

1.

Parse
(G{},(X_{3},D_{3}))=V.

2.

Define the oracle
\mathcal{O}_{3}^{\prime}
: On input
ivk,y_{0},y_{1},m
, output
D_{3}(m)
if
ivk,y_{0},y_{1},m\in X_{3}
, otherwise output
\bot
.

3.

Simulate

to obtain
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{internal}},S
.

4.

Output
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{internal}},S
.
